Direct polarisation-state reconstruction with a temporal pointer: accessible states, resolution and optimal coupling

Weak measurements are used to amplify small signals, to address foundational questions, and to characterise quantum states without tomography. In direct state reconstruction each amplitude of the state vector is proportional to a single complex weak value, which for a temporal pointer is read from the mean arrival time and the mean frequency of the postselected pulse. Such an experiment requires three quantities to be fixed, the coupling, the angular resolution demanded of the reconstruction, and the set of states over which the method remains usable, and these are commonly set by the available apparatus rather than derived. We derive all three for a Gaussian temporal pointer and an arbitrary postselection state. The states that cannot be reconstructed form a spherical cap centred on the state orthogonal to the postselection, whose geodesic half-angle follows from the largest of three independent thresholds and involves no adjustable parameter. Two states are distinguishable only if they differ by more than a minimal step fixed by the coupling, by the number of incident photons and by the sensitivity of the weak value along the path followed. For an inversion at first order in the coupling the systematic and statistical errors are opposed and fix an optimal coupling that varies as the inverse sixth root of the number of incident photons, whereas an exact inversion at finite coupling removes the systematic error, the remaining bound being one on the two-centroid readout rather than on the inversion, since below an amplitude equal to a quarter of the coupling two states give the same pair of centroids while their profiles differ. These results allow a direct reconstruction experiment to be dimensioned before the apparatus is assembled.

Exact Gaussian--Hermite closure of the mapping quantum--classical Liouville equation

In the Meyer--Miller--Stock--Thoss mapping representation, the Liouville operator of the quantum--classical Liouville equation contains an excess coupling term in addition to the Poisson bracket. Neglecting this term yields the Poisson bracket mapping equation (PBME). We expand the mapping dependence of the density in Hermite functions about the Gaussian factor common to all physical mapping densities. In this basis, the excess coupling term acts purely as a raising operator and exactly cancels the raising contribution generated by the back--reaction force. Consequently, the exact dynamics does not generate higher Hermite orders and closes on a finite, sparse system of equations for the coefficient fields. Within the physical sector, these equations reduce to the subsystem quantum--classical Liouville equation. In contrast, the PBME retains the raising contribution of the force, modifying the back reaction within the physical sector while continuously generating higher Hermite orders. For Tully's dual avoided crossing model, direct propagation of the coefficient equations confirms this finite closure and agrees with independent solutions of the subsystem equation. Comparison with converged full quantum dynamics further shows that, across all regimes examined, the PBME is less accurate than the quantum--classical description for populations, coherences, and momentum distributions.

Geodesic steering for rapid dissipative entanglement distribution

We describe an optimal-control strategy designed to accelerate the dissipative preparation of complex, highly entangled quantum many-body states. Analogous to optimal steering of isolated quantum systems, our scheme identifies control pulses that guide the evolution of open quantum systems along trajectories of instantaneous steady states while minimizing leakage resulting from non-adiabatic transitions. In many cases of interest, these control parameters can be derived analytically and without the need for computationally expensive numerical simulations of the full many-body master equation. We apply this technique for the preparation of bi- and multipartite entangled states in qubit systems coupled to a common photonic waveguide or a distributed squeezed reservoir. In both scenarios, our optimal-control strategy significantly outperforms the bare relaxation dynamics after a quench, reducing preparation times of near-maximally entangled states by orders of magnitude. This drastic speedup makes dissipative entanglement distribution protocols compatible with high-fidelity quantum information processing applications and illustrates more broadly the potential of leakage minimization for accelerating reservoir-engineering schemes.

The Quantum Character Transform

Bastidas et al. (2025) highlighted the importance of exploiting group symmetries to reduce computational overhead and unify simulation methods across both condensed matter physics and ab initio quantum chemistry. I discuss the discrete character transform, which is the group Fourier transform restricted to $Z(\mathbb{C}[G])$, the center of the group algebra, where class functions form a commutative convolution algebra and irreducible characters are its spectral basis. The normalized character table of a non-abelian group can be obtained through a restriction operator, which means an efficient quantum character transform for any finite group with an efficient quantum Fourier transform. I give complementary quantum algorithms for class convolution based on direct class-sum block encoding and spectral implementation in the character basis, explaining their applications to symmetry-adapted many-body dynamics, molecular and spectroscopic symmetry analysis, and quantum walks, as well as characterizing the central information used in hidden-subgroup problems, including its limitations for graph isomorphism.

QMBL: the Quantum Many-Body Leaderboard. A live record of published ground-state energies for lattice Hamiltonians and the electron gas

In condensed matter physics, scientists explore phase diagrams of matter through various methods (analysis, numerics, experiment) and achieve consensus when enough methods agree. The quality of variational results for quantum many-body ground states is mainly judged by energy values and respective errors. Here, we present QMBL, the Quantum Many-Body Leaderboard (https://www.qmbl.org), serving as the energy layer of the scientific consensus. It is a maintained, interactive record of published ground-state energies for lattice Hamiltonians and the homogeneous electron gas. The latest release v0.5.0 holds 2511 energies on 532 instances and collects all of the community's efforts into one view. Of those instances 285 have no exact solution (164 lattice and all 121 electron-gas instances) and any new record serves as a direct improvement to an open science problem. Realistic simulation of universal multiport interferometers with losses within a Gaussian formalism

Photonic technologies are among the leading platform contenders for demonstrating quantum advantage. Nevertheless, photon losses represent the major hurdle for the realization of a fault-tolerant optical quantum computer, and the need for an accurate characterization of this phenomenon is crucial for classical simulations of quantum algorithms on noisy backends. The noisy regime would require the simulation of the algorithm on a Fock backend or rather assuming the a priori hypothesis on the uniformity of the optical losses, which may lead to wrong predictions about the outcome from the algorithm on a cerain hardware. We propose a Gaussian model that can reproduce a lossy dynamics through the experimental characterization of imperfect interferometers, yielding the corresponding mean vector and the covariance matrix. Such model can be adapted to any continuous-variable architecture (spatial or time-bin encoded) and for both the Gaussian boson sampling or general-dyne based algorithms. Eventually, we quantify the drift of optical losses from being uniform throughout the multiport interferometer by a numerical simulation. This work proposes a blueprint for future characterizations, tailor-suited for specific devices and architectures, as well as for potential error correction and mitigation techniques, and it paves the way for realistic simulations of quantum algorithms on NISQ optical platforms.

Fisher-Information Certification from Bell Statistics: CHSH Bounds, Information Loss, and Finite-Data Guarantees

Bell-test data are typically used to certify nonlocal correlations, while quantum metrology is concerned with the sensitivity of measurement statistics to unknown parameters. Here we connect these tasks at the level of experimentally accessible Bell statistics. Using standard moment-based Fisher-information inequalities, we derive lower bounds on the classical Fisher information directly from Bell scores and their parameter dependence, without requiring full state tomography. For CHSH measurements we establish a hierarchy between the information contained in the full outcome record, the set of individual correlators, and the compressed Bell parameter, thereby quantifying the metrological cost of data compression. In polarization-phase sensing, this hierarchy reveals a Bell-metrology tradeoff: the point of maximal CHSH violation need not maximize, and can even suppress, the Fisher information carried by the Bell score itself. We further derive finite-sample lower-confidence bounds from coincidence counts and analyze the effects of biased marginals, accidental coincidences, visibility loss, and multipair emission. The framework provides a practical route for extracting metrological guarantees from Bell-type experiments using only standard correlation measurements.

Euler's thirty-six officers need not all be entangled: real AME(4,6), families and classification

Absolutely maximally entangled states of four six-level systems, AME$(4,6)$, are the quantum solutions of Euler's problem of the thirty-six officers. Until recently all reported solutions were complex, and it was conjectured that no real orthogonal 2-unitary matrix of order $36$ exists. We give real AME$(4,6)$ states with $60$ non-zero coefficients in $\{\pm1,\sqrt2\}/\sqrt{72}$. Of their thirty-six officers, $12$ are classical, as some must be whenever fewer than $72$ coefficients are non-zero. They lie in the $-1$ eigenspace of an order-$4$ local symmetry, and on their support the AME conditions become linear. We derive two closed-form families of AME$(4,6)$ states that meet at one of these states: a $10$-parameter family that is supported on $72$ basis states, and an $11$-parameter family on $60$ basis states. At each of its two ends the first family loses $12$ coefficients, and the AME states on the remaining $60$ basis states form an $11$-parameter family: the second family at one end, and an analogous family at the other. These two $60$-term families contain $4096$ real AME states up to diagonal local unitaries, $1024$ of which are limits of the first family. They fall into exactly $20$ classes under local unitaries and permutations of the parties, and we certify their separation with interval arithmetic.

Reverse quantum aging: Restoring rhythmicity from quantum aging state

Quantum aging transition has recently been reported, whereby the oscillatory dynamics of a network of globally coupled quantum self-sustained oscillators is degraded sharply when the fraction of inactive nodes exceeds a critical value. Here we explore the reverse process: the revival of rhythmic dynamics from an aged state in a quantum oscillator network by introducing a controlled incoherent single-boson pumping channel. Using a self-consistent quantum master equation in the Lindblad form for globally coupled active--inactive quantum Stuart--Landau oscillators, we show that increasing the pumping strength progressively suppresses the aging transition and eventually eliminates it, restoring rhythmicity even when all oscillators are inactive. Remarkably, complete compensation of the coupling-induced dissipative loss is not required; a substantially weaker pumping strength is sufficient to eliminate the aging transition. We show that the revival is governed by a redistribution of the Fock-state population of the inactive oscillators toward higher-occupation states. Our results establish controlled incoherent pumping as an effective mechanism for restoring dynamical robustness in quantum oscillator networks.

Bosonic Quantum Conditional Mutual Information and Bose-Einstein Condensation

This article aims to introduce the bosonic asymmetric quantum conditional mutual information (QCMI), $I_{B}(A; B|C)$, as a rigorously defined information-theoretic quantity for continuous-variable (bosonic) systems. After defining $I_{B}(A; B|C)$ through quantum instruments on infinite-dimensional Fock spaces, we prove that Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) induces a logarithmic divergence $ I_{B}(A; B|C) \geq α\log_2 (N_{0}^C+1)$, which further establishes $I_{B}(A; B|C)$ as a quantum-information order parameter for BEC with power-law scaling near the critical point. We provide the complete five-step proof of this result, connecting measurement back-action, off-diagonal long-range order, and Jensen's inequality. In the condensed phase, the effective causal propagation velocity is set by the Bogoliubov sound speed $c_s$ in the long-wavelength limit, and vanishes continuously at the superfluid--Mott-insulator transition. Applications to composite bosons, the BEC--BCS crossover, and a quantum-gas-microscope measurement protocol are presented. Finally, we demonstrate how the reduced bosonic QCMI serves as a finite-size scaling probe analogous to the Binder cumulant.

Emergent quantum algebras in dissipative time crystals

Strong dynamical symmetries provide an algebraic description of persistent oscillations in open quantum systems governed by the Lindblad equation. For finite-dimensional systems with a full-rank stationary state, such symmetries are necessary and sufficient for non-decaying oscillatory modes to appear in the Liouvillian's spectrum. These modes can be directly constructed by acting on stationary states with the identified symmetry operators. Here we establish an asymptotic counterpart of this algebra for collective spins. Taking a single spin $S$ in a magnetic field, subject to dissipation through linear jump operators, we identify the exact conditions on the field and dissipative couplings under which non-decaying oscillatory modes emerge as $S \rightarrow \infty$. We then write down a simple, closed form for the corresponding `symmetry' operator which can be applied to the steady state to construct these modes, which are eigenvectors of the Liouvillian, with imaginary eigenvalues, up to $O(1/S)$ corrections. A second operator, an emergent conserved quantity, completes the algebra, and its slow relaxation determines how quantum noise selects the stationary state. The construction can be directly extended to a pair of interacting spins sharing a common reservoir, with the resulting quantum algebra making the entanglement during a quench analytically accessible.

Towards the Geometry of Quantum Optical Measurements: Kraus Trajectories in the Instrumental Weyl-Heisenberg Group Manifold

Since ancient Greek times, geometry has been a method by which humans can make inferences, by the use of similar triangles, about the world far beyond their immediate senses. Similarity plays an equally important role in quantum measurement, except that instead of triangles, the similarity is between the infinitesimal generators of rotation, canonical transformation, or some other virtual symmetry. For many quantum optical measurements of a single mode, these generators produce Kraus operators that are contained within a twelve-dimensional Lie group: the Instrumental Weyl-Heisenberg group manifold ($\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$). In particular, $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ is general enough to include all diffusively generated 'dyne measurements. In this paper, $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ will be used to develop two more general classes of 'dyne measurement: one that interpolates between diffusive homodyne and diffusive heterodyne (``slomodyne'') and one that adds to 'dyne measurements adaptive squeezing and displacement. Our analysis uses an approach known as the Instrument Manifold Program (IMP). In particular, a Harish-Chandra-like decomposition of $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ is used to calculate the vielbein transformation from a right-invariant basis to the corresponding coordinate basis, allowing us to universally and autonomously navigate $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ and locate the Kraus-operator of whatever selective process is being considered as it evolves in time according to the measurement record.
---
Bell's go theorem: regaining local causality with a new notion of realism

Bell's theorem is popularly stated as the inconsistency of quantum theory with local realism. Realism is here formalized by the ontological models framework, where elements of reality are points of a measurable space and probabilities obey the Kolmogorov axioms. In the Bell scenario, locally causal ontological models posit a common cause supporting a global valuation, a joint assignment of outcomes to all measurements at once, across incompatible contexts. Within this notion of realism, Bell's theorem makes any realist explanation of the quantum predictions conspiratorial: any mechanism, whether superluminal, superdeterministic, or retrocausal, must be finely tuned so as never to manifest operationally. We redefine realism through a new ontological framework that generalizes the standard one. The elements of reality, which we call records, are no longer points but relational parts of a point-free generalization of a topological space (sublocales of a locale). A record shared by two incompatible contexts establishes the sameness of a measurement across them, without such pairwise identities composing into a global valuation, and the common cause is then a web of such records. Within this framework, Bell's no-go theorem becomes a go theorem: local causality, restated for records, is no longer at odds with the quantum statistics. This is possible because modularity, the inclusion-exclusion law of classical probability, fails across incompatible contexts, so that the probability theory generalizes Kolmogorov's. We exhibit explicit models reproducing several quantum strategies, including the optimal CHSH violation. More broadly, locally causal models of this kind attain every Bell-CHSH value up to the algebraic maximum. A realist explanation of the quantum predictions need not be conspiratorial; every causal influence posited by these models is reflected in the operational predictions.

Quantum state texture of maximal-resource Fourier states under Hawking radiation

Quantum state texture (QST) is a recently proposed quantum resource that characterizes the nonuniformity of a density matrix in a given computational basis. In the resource theory of QST, the Fourier states f_k are maximally resourceful states, attaining the maximum QST measure. We study the QST behavior of three maximally resourceful states in a two-mode Dirac field under Hawking radiation from a Schwarzschild black hole, using a fidelity-based measure. We consider three cases: only mode B undergoes radiation, only mode A undergoes radiation, and both modes undergo radiation simultaneously. Our results show that for single-mode radiation, if the mode unaffected by radiation is orthogonal to the single-mode textureless state, the QST of the overall output state remains maximal and is independent of the Hawking temperature; we call this phenomenon orthogonality protection. When both modes undergo radiation simultaneously, orthogonality protection vanishes, and the QST of all three Fourier states decreases with increasing Hawking temperature. Notably, the QST measure of f_3 under two-mode radiation is higher than that under only mode B radiation, indicating that QST can be redistributed among modes: QST gained by one mode under radiation can partially compensate for QST lost by the other. We further provide a general resource-theoretic explanation of orthogonality protection, showing that this phenomenon is independent of the specific form of the QST measure. These results reveal how QST degrades under Hawking radiation and provide a new perspective on the redistribution of quantum resources in black hole backgrounds.

Deterministic positioning accuracy of B centers in hBN by electron irradiation

Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is a promising material platform for quantum technologies, enabling both the realization of nanophotonic devices and the hosting of point defects that act as high-quality single-photon emitters (SPEs). Among these defects, B centers, which reproducibly emit at 436 nm, can be generated individually at predefined positions using electron beam irradiation, a crucial capability for top-down quantum photonics. Yet, the spatial accuracy of emitter creation strongly conditions the potential applications. Here, we generate arrays of individual B centers in a scanning electron microscope (SEM) with in-situ cathodoluminescence (CL) monitoring. We characterize the sources of random deviations from the target positions to identify the limitations to their deterministic positioning. In particular, we measure the distance between the electron beam and the emitter at the moment the emitter is created based on CL mapping. This approach provides a direct estimation of the spatial extent of the beam-emitter interaction. We find this contribution to be the main constraint to the emitter fabrication accuracy, yielding a standard deviation of 27 nm. Additional contributions, primarily from the finite density of UV-emitting carbon dimer precursors, increase the overall positional standard deviation to 40 nm. Our results highlight the possible improvements to the B center creation technique to reach sub-10 nm accuracy.

Magic-Free Certification of Logical Error Rates in Universal Fault-Tolerant Circuits

As quantum hardware enters the fault-tolerant regime, algorithms are beginning to be executed on encoded qubits, yet scalable certification of how well they run remains an open problem. Here we introduce magic-free certification, a certification protocol for universal fault-tolerant circuits that consumes no magic, the resource that generally places them beyond efficient classical simulation and is by far the most expensive element of a fault-tolerant computation. The protocol is code- and decoder-agnostic, runs interleaved with the target circuit on the same device, and has a sample complexity that grows linearly with the number of logical qubits and non-Clifford gates while remaining decoupled from the logical error rate itself. Given a predefined error tolerance $p_L^*$, the protocol determines at a chosen confidence level whether the device executes the target circuit at a logical error rate below $p_L^*$. The method relies on trap circuits, copies of the target with every magic state replaced by a stabilizer state, whose outcomes are deterministic and which, being indistinguishable from the target to stochastic Pauli noise, fail at the same rate. We illustrate the protocol in Steane-code simulations of a magic state injection gadget and a quantum Fourier transform.

A central goal of quantum machine learning is to identify applications in which small quantum circuits can improve task performance while remaining executable on real hardware. We investigate deepfake (synthetic) speech detection, a largely unexplored application in this area, where reliable confidence scores are as important as discrimination performance because deployment decisions are made at fixed thresholds and overconfident errors can be costly. The quantum circuit yields better-calibrated confidence in most matched comparisons, including out-of-distribution evaluation, though the downstream deployment-cost benefit is model-dependent. Together, these results show that hardware-executable quantum bottlenecks can improve score reliability, with gains that vary by evaluation setting.

Entanglement and parametric resonance in coupled oscillator systems

We study a system of two quadratically-coupled harmonic oscillators, prepared respectively in the ground state and in a coherent state. This toy model can describe for instance the dynamics of one mode of a quantum field evolving in a harmonically-oscillating classical background. In such situations, it is well-known that, depending on the background oscillation amplitude and coupling strength, the system may exhibit parametric resonance. Using the Feynman-Vernon influence functional, we analyze the interplay between parametric resonance and quantum entanglement by perturbatively computing the purity of the mixed state resulting from tracing over one of the oscillators. We find a runaway behavior of the quantum entanglement between the two oscillators, as well as a rapid breakdown of the validity of perturbation theory, in regions of parametric instability. This provides a quantitative confirmation of various statements in the literature and is highly relevant to questions pertaining to the validity of the semiclassical approximation in coupled quantum-classical systems.

Benchmarking a randomized quantum algorithm for estimating expectation values of molecular observables

Randomized quantum algorithms for the estimation of expectation values of molecular observables offer a promising route to early fault-tolerant quantum advantage by trading circuit depth for an increased number of samples. However, this begs the question whether the resulting measurement overhead remains feasible for industrially relevant molecular systems. In this work, we benchmark the randomized sampling algorithm of Wang et al. [1] through numerical simulations on a hydrogen molecule and resource estimates for various active spaces of the cytochrome P450, an industry-relevant system in the pharmaceutical industry. Our numerical simulations reveal that the analytical error bounds significantly overestimate the actual sampling error, whereas the analytical bound on the required imaginary evolution time is comparatively tight, exceeding a direct DMRG estimate by roughly 44% for the active space where the comparison is feasible. The measurement overhead nevertheless remains prohibitively high: even for the smallest active spaces, the expectation-value estimation alone requires around 1010 samples, scaling to beyond 1015 for the largest active spaces considered. These results suggest that substantial improvements in measurement strategies or algorithmic scaling are necessary before randomized early-fault-tolerant algorithms become competitive for industrial quantum chemistry applications.

Pathfinder-BP: A Low-Latency Recursive Decoder for Topological Codes

We propose Pathfinder-BP, a recursive decoder for topological quantum codes that combines belief propagation with controlled bit-flipping. An initial BP pass identifies and permanently fixes high-confidence errors. Upon convergence failure, the decoder recursively explores alternative error patterns by tentatively flipping low-confidence variables. On surface codes, Pathfinder-BP achieves a noise threshold and logical error rate comparable to minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM). A crucial advantage of Pathfinder-BP is its highly parallelizable structure, which yields a worst-case decoding latency quasi-linear in the code distance, making it a suitable candidate for low-latency hardware implementation.

Finite-time quantum equilibration from local correlations

An isolated quantum system evolves reversibly, yet a small subsystem can lose nearly all information about its initial state. Conserved quantities may retain some of this information, depending on how strongly they overlap with local observables. Here we consider a subsystem in an arbitrary initial state coupled to a maximally mixed bath. We show that the worst-input mean-square equilibration error, maximised over window origins, is bounded from above and below by the mean square of a complete local autocorrelation, with the stationary part subtracted. The two mean-square quantities are linearly comparable. A correlation record over a finite interval therefore controls the equilibration error in every interval of the same length, and the result extends to nonnegative observation weights with nonnegative Fourier transform. The result allows arbitrary degeneracies of energies and gaps. We apply it to two static Hamiltonian families. For fixed spin subsystems in the one-particle sector of a bounded-range periodic clock, fixed-accuracy thermalisation is guaranteed on windows that grow only slightly faster than linearly with system size, despite exponentially small positive energies. A random commuting model with logarithmic interaction range retains an extensive set of conserved observables. Its expected mean-square error is bounded by a finite-size term and an inverse-time term. The inverse-time dependence is unavoidable because of the initial transient. These examples show how local relaxation can coexist with exact conservation laws and a finely spaced spectrum.

Nested polynomial algebras and the exact spectrum of a three-dimensional two-spin oscillator

We construct representations of the symmetry algebra and derive the exact spectrum of a three-dimensional oscillator with two spin-$\tfrac12$ degrees of freedom. The Hamiltonian and its first-order scalar and pseudo-scalar integrals were obtained in earlier classifications. Here we determine their action on the complete spectral basis. At fixed total angular momentum, a quadratic algebra acts on the three triplet orbital channels and admits a three-dimensional deformed-oscillator representation, while an overlapping two-state exchange algebra joins the middle triplet to the singlet. These finite modules determine angular--spin multiplicities but do not quantize the radial energy. A separate positive-discrete-series $su(1,1)$ representation of the common radial Hamiltonian generates the energy sequence. We give the finite matrices, Casimir and structure functions, normalized eigenfunctions, radial ladder actions, the exceptional $j=0$ sector, complete energy-shell multiplicities, and the effect of particle interchange.

Metasurface quantum graphs for polarization-independent certified randomness

Quantum random number generators are crucial for information security, yet existing implementations are hindered by bulky optics, device imperfections, and state alignment constraints. Here, using a compact metasurface platform, we achieve polarization-independent certified randomness by establishing a quantitative quantum graph framework. By mapping multi-channel nanophotonic responses onto graph vertices, we assign metasurface measurements an operational information-theoretic meaning, directly connecting weighted graph degrees to conditional min-entropy estimation. The resulting graph-certified measurements yield robust private randomness even under entanglement-based attacks without complex optics. Experimentally, we fabricate 4- and 6-channel dielectric metasurfaces to implement high-dimensional positive operator-valued measure graphs, successfully certifying randomness with heralded single photons and Poissonian light. Continuity of certain quantum channel divergences

The geometric channel fidelity, the geometric Rényi divergences and the Belavkin--Staszewski relative entropy give converse bounds for capacities, discrimination and estimation. Channels are known only approximately, yet two pairs of channels with equal sums can be arbitrarily close in the diamond norm and have fidelities $0$ and $1$. The remedy is the Arveson--Radon--Nikodym derivative $T$ of one map with respect to the sum $Ω$, whose transpose is the environment effect selecting that map in a Stinespring dilation of $Ω$. In $T$, these quantities and all maximal $f$-divergences of operator convex $f$ are continuous, with explicit dimension-free moduli; for two pairs of channels with the same sum the fidelities differ by at most $2\|T_1-T_2\|_\infty^{1/2}$, which gives Hölder continuity in the diamond norm on such splittings. The fidelity is continuous at a pair of completely positive maps exactly when the Choi matrix of the sum is invertible or the fidelity vanishes: noisy channels of full Kraus rank are continuity points, unitary gates are not.

Classical and Quantum Information Measures Characterizing Convertibility: An Axiomatic Approach

In this PhD thesis, we take an axiomatic approach to studying multivariate divergences, which are measures of the information content of a tuple of classical or quantum states. These divergences are studied in the context of large-sample and catalytic convertibility of tuples of states. This type of convertibility is characterized by the ordering of an axiomatically defined family of divergences. In this thesis, we identify this family of divergences explicitly for two scenarios, one classical and one quantum. In the classical setting, we show that the relevant divergences are certain generalizations of the bivariate Rényi divergences. Unlike previous studies, we do not require the distributions to have the same support, yielding interesting new divergences. In the quantum setting, we consider pairs of classical-quantum states with pure component states, and find that majorization is characterized by all $α$-$z$ relative entropies for $α<1$. This demonstrates the first operational interpretation of these relative entropies.

Minimal Subsystems for Characterizing Genuine Multipartite Entanglement

Genuine multipartite entanglement (GME) is a valuable resource for quantum technologies, but quantifying it in many-body systems can be demanding since measures defined through optimizations over bipartitions are often assumed to require large joint measurements and many measurement settings. We introduce the concepts of GME evaluation length and GME evaluation number, which capture, respectively, the largest subsystem that must be jointly measured and the number of distinct subsystems that need to be measured to evaluate multipartite entanglement measures defined via bipartition optimizations. Taking the genuine multipartite concurrence (GMC) as a canonical example, we show that, for $N$-qubit Haar-random pure states, the GMC can be evaluated using only $N$ single-qubit purity measurements. Thus, for typical many-body states, evaluating the GMC requires considering only the $N$ single-site bipartitions, rather than the exponentially many distinct bipartitions. We further corroborate this behavior numerically for physically accessible states generated by local random quantum circuits. Our results show that the practical cost of quantifying GME can be dramatically lower than suggested by its formal definition, establishing measurement complexity as a complementary operational lens for multipartite entanglement.

Collective Floquet engineering between two distant superconducting artificial atoms

A one-dimensional waveguide is a crucial medium for transferring propagating quantum information between remote qubits, yet the photon-mediated interaction is typically fixed by the static device geometry, qubit parameters, and their couplings to the transmission line. Such an interaction can be engineered to be coherent or dissipative via virtual- or real-photon exchange, respectively, yet its effective strength remains fixed in either case. Here we demonstrate dynamical control over this interaction by applying Floquet engineering to two superconducting qubits coupled to a waveguide terminated by a capacitive mirror. By positioning one qubit at an antinode and the other qubit at a node or an antinode of the resonant standing wave, we realize a Hermitian or non-Hermitian coupling between the two qubits. A periodic flux drive of the qubit frequencies then enables on-demand control of the coherent energy splitting and the collective decay. Our results establish Floquet engineering as a route to dynamically reshape both coherent and dissipative photon-mediated interactions between remote quantum emitters.

Bootstrapping Classical and Quantum Query Lower Bounds for Finding Tarski Fixed Points

The Tarski fixed-point problem asks for a fixed point of a monotone map on a $k$-dimensional grid lattice of width $n$ and has applications in game theory, economics, and verification. Substantial gaps remain between the known upper and lower bounds on its quantum query complexity.
  We prove two hardness bootstrapping theorems for quantum query lower bounds. Starting from a hard instance family whose instances have pair-wise incomparable unique fixed points, and a lower bound established by the nonnegative adversary method, we bootstrap this lower bound by (i) a factor of $Ω(t)$ when the dimension is multiplied by $t$ and the width remains unchanged, and (ii) a factor of $Ω(\log m)$ when the width is multiplied by $m$ and the dimension is doubled. Applying these theorems, we obtain three consequences: (i) an $Ω(k\log^2 n)$ lower bound for $k\geq 4$, improving previously best-known classical and quantum lower bounds; (ii) for every fixed constant $α>0$, there exists a constant $δ(α)>0$ such that $Q_ε(\text{Tarski}(n,k))=Ω(kn^{δ(α)})$ whenever $k>n^α$, and in particular, an $Ω(kn^{0.0072})$ lower bound for $k>n^{2/203}$, the first lower bound with simultaneous linear dependence on the dimension and polynomial dependence on the width; and (iii) a superpolynomial lower bound of $(k/\log k)^{Ω(\log k)}$ for exponentially wide grids, where $n=2^{Θ(k)}$. The last result rules out a route to polynomial-time algorithms for simple stochastic games and ARRIVAL through generic polynomial-query black-box Tarski solvers.

Deterministic preparation of squeezed and non-Gaussian states in coupled qubit-boson systems

Deterministic preparation of bosonic resource states is a central task in continuous-variable quantum information processing and quantum metrology. Here we develop a measurement-based imaginary time evolution (MITE) protocol for preparing squeezed and non-Gaussian states. The protocol combines ancilla-mediated weak measurements of the Hamiltonian with a conditional phase rotation that suppresses excited state trapping and stabilizes convergence toward the ground state. The method is potentially applicable in hybrid discrete variable-continuous variable (DV-CV) platforms, where the qubit acts as the measurement readout. We demonstrate deterministic convergence over a broad range of parameters and show that the performance degradation near the stability boundary is governed by the shrinking Bogoliubov quasiparticle gap. The same framework also extends to non-Gaussian bosonic resource states, including Schrödinger cat states and excited states of the single-mode squeezing Hamiltonian, which display Wigner negativity. The squeezed states remain robust under bosonic loss over experimentally relevant regimes.

Time-Frequency Continuous-Variable Quantum Teleportation with Single Photons

Quantum teleportation, a key building block for quantum technologies, enables distant parties, Alice and Bob, to transfer an unknown quantum state using entanglement, local operations, and classical communication. In this work, we propose a quantum teleportation protocol for single-photon time-frequency continuous variables, where time is the time of arrival at a detector. We detail the operational requirements of the protocol, notably the nonlinear interactions needed for the photon-photon time-frequency entangling gate. By explicitly treating the detector position as the reference frame for time of arrival, we show that, even with ideal classical and quantum channels, the input-output fidelity is not unity unless the receiving party can correct the detector-position mismatch. This results in a trade-off between teleportation fidelity and the temporal bandwidth of the input photon wavepacket. The protocol itself can be used for remote detector synchronization. In particular, we show that time-frequency cat and Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill states provide an internal temporal ruler through fidelity revivals. We further derive the classical and best-copy fidelity thresholds for single-photon Gaussian wavepackets, finding that the relevant vacuum-equivalent shot noise is input-dependent, in contrast with quadrature-based continuous-variable quantum teleportation. Our proposal derives from a quantum theory of space-time events, whereafter we establish space as a reference for the time degree of freedom, thereby grounding our work in ongoing research on reference frames and superselection rules in quantum optics and quantum information. Beyond protocols harnessing time-frequency continuous variables of single photons, this work points toward remote synchronization in quantum communication architectures.

Probe Signatures of Quantum Chaos with Quantum Sensing

The spectral form factor (SFF), which is the Fourier transform of the two-point spectral correlation, is a fundamental diagnostics of quantum chaos. Nevertheless, its experimental detection in quantum many-body systems was less unexplored until recently. Here, we establish a relationship between the SFF with the quantum Fisher information (QFI) by means of quantum sensing protocols. Furthermore, when the eigenvalues and eigenstates of the disordered Hamiltonian are statistically independent, we prove that QFI is typical both in short and long timescales while numerics suggest that it is also typical at intermediate timescales, which ensures self-averaging of QFI. The self-averaging property renders the experimental measurement of QFI with our protocol less noisy in contrast with the direct measurement of SFF which is known to be non-self-averaging. Our results reveal an intriguing connection between quantum noise and the signatures of quantum chaos.

Architectural Robustness to Label Noise Using Variational Quantum Circuits for Medical Image Classification

Label noise is prevalent in medical image datasets, arising from observer disagreement and ambiguous cases and deep networks memorize the mislabeled examples at the cost of generalization. Current solutions such as loss correction and sample selection add training complexity and generally assume the noise rate is known or estimable. This paper investigates whether robustness can be obtained architecturally, by routing a frozen backbone's features through a low-dimensional bounded bottleneck and classifying them with a head whose function class is intrinsically smooth. Variational quantum circuits with angle encoding and a linear cost function realise exactly such a class. Their use in medical imaging, however, has been limited by the small number of qubits available on noisy intermediate-scale devices, which typically restricts inputs to low-resolution grayscale images. We therefore couple a frozen ImageNet-pretrained ViT-B/16 backbone to a trainable linear bottleneck and an eight-qubit variational quantum circuit, allowing 224x224 RGB inputs to be processed. Under clean labels the model leads five of six MedMNISTv2 benchmarks against published hybrid quantum-classical models, with margins of 17.4 and 15.7 percentages on BloodMNIST and PathMNIST. The advantage emerges under corrupted labels. At symmetric noise rates of 30% and 50% the model outperforms many published quantum and noise-robust classical baselines on all six datasets without any explicit noise-handling component. Against a control that shares the backbone, bottleneck, parameter count and optimizer and replaces only the circuit with a ReLU layer, it retains 89.75% accuracy on PathMNIST at 50% noise where the control falls to 78.96%. Direct measurement of training dynamics shows that this advantage is not an inability to fit corrupted labels, which the circuit retains, but greater stability under prolonged training on them.

Device-to-protocol simulation of spectrally-resolved quantum photonic circuits

Predicting quantum-photonic circuit performance requires retaining source and device physics without making photon-counting calculations prohibitively expensive. Here we present PhoQuS, a framework that combines frequency-resolved source models, electromagnetic compact models and spatially correlated fabrication variation to simulate quantum photonic circuits. Covariance-Gram reduction after full-resolution Gaussian propagation lowers readout cost while retaining dominant spectral correlations. For continuous-variable state preparation, spectral reduction accelerates photon counting by 60.4-fold in an approximate Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill circuit. Frequency-resolved modeling guides the use of cascaded squeezing with temporal walk-off compensation, yielding 0.99 fidelity to the single-mode circuit reference and 0.98 conditional purity in lossless simulations. PhoQuS connects device physics to protocol-level performance through a unified workflow that retains spectral detail while reducing readout cost.

This basis is not perfectly distinguishable by LOCC or even by separable measurements when a single copy of the unknown state is available. Few-Magnon Universality beyond Contact Interactions in Long-Range Spin Chains

Quantum few-body systems exhibit a rich variety of phenomena that provide fundamental insights into universality. A paradigmatic example is the Efimov effect, in which an infinite tower of three-body bound states emerges with binding energies forming a geometric sequence. Motivated by advances in trapped-ion systems, recent theoretical work has predicted that the Efimov effect can arise in certain long-range quantum spin chains, where magnons exhibit a general dispersion $ε_k=u|k|^z$ with $z\in(0.52,0.88)$ and interact through a contact potential. Nevertheless, whether Efimov universality persists in the presence of experimentally relevant long-range interactions between magnons remains unexplored. In this work, we address this issue by investigating the two- and three-magnon problems with a model interaction potential $V(x)=V_0/(1+|x|^β)$. Our results demonstrate that few-body universality persists for any $β>1$: In the two-magnon sector, the low-energy expansion of the scattering matrix near a two-body resonance agrees with the universal form predicted by contact-interaction theory. The three-magnon bound-state spectrum exhibits Efimov scaling, with a scaling factor consistent with the theory. We further highlight the experimental relevance of our model, which can be readily realized in trapped-ion systems. Our results pave the way for quantum simulations of universal few-body physics beyond the traditional paradigm of nonrelativistic particles.

QIAP: A Quantum-Inspired Authentication Protocol with BB84-Based Channel Monitoring

Authentication in quantum-aware communication settings requires mechanisms that verify user legitimacy while also assessing whether the communication channel exhibits excessive disturbance. This paper presents QIAP, a stateful hybrid framework that combines classical commitment-based challenge-response authentication with BB84-based quantum-channel monitoring. QIAP uses a dual-branch verification structure inspired by rectilinear and diagonal basis selection. In the rectilinear branch, the prover opens a hash-based commitment and the verifier checks consistency with the registered round token. In the diagonal branch, the prover returns an HMAC tag bound to the verifier nonce, round identifier, and submitted commitment without disclosing the round-specific sub-secret. Under the stated hash, HMAC, freshness, secure-registration, protected-storage, and high-entropy-secret assumptions, QIAP provides computational resistance to the modeled wrong-secret and replay attempts and protects the long-term master secret from direct recovery. After successful authentication, the BB84 module estimates the quantum bit error rate (QBER) and aborts the session when the measured value reaches or exceeds the configured threshold. In the reported simulation, no successful authentication was observed among 1,000 tested wrong-secret attempts per branch. The baseline and full intercept-resend conditions produced mean QBER values of approximately 0.02 and 0.25, respectively. The classical authentication stage required approximately 0.6 ms, and the implemented authentication messages required approximately 113 bytes per round. These results indicate that QIAP can provide a lightweight hybrid framework for combining secret-based identity verification with QBER-based channel-disturbance assessment under the stated threat model.

Noise-resistant off-resonance π-rotation realized in an NMR system

Precise manipulation of qubits (spins) requires strong suppression of noise arising from couplings between qubits and their environment, as well as from driving field fluctuations. We propose a double-pulse sequence and an eighteen-pulse sequence to realize a single spin pi-rotation under off-resonance conditions (instead of conventional on-resonance or near-resonance conditions). It is proved that the latter sequence performs much better than the former in simultaneously suppressing both dephasing and driving field noise. As a proof of principle, we experimentally demonstrate, by implementing both sequences in an NMR spin system with a polarized initial spin state, that the off-resonance pi-rotation fidelity reaches as high as 0.9992 +/- 0.0008 for the double-pulse sequence and 0.9997 +/- 0.0006 for the eighteen-pulse sequence. In addition, the noise-tolerance range of the eighteen-pulse sequence is about 20 percent of the driving field strength, approximately three times wider than that of the double-pulse sequence. Besides its potential application in the NMR community, our proposal provides an alternative practical way to accurately control qubits by suppressing the coexisting transversal and longitudinal noises.

From isolated singular wells to exact Bloch bands: the trigonometric Pöschl-Teller lattice

An exactly solvable lattice with periodic inverse-square singularities exhibits two physically distinct mechanisms of spectral-gap closure. The model arises from the generalized trigonometric Pöschl-Teller potential in the coupling range where both local solutions at each singularity are square-integrable. A transmitting, current-preserving self-adjoint matching condition connects neighboring cells and generates a nontrivial Bloch spectrum. For a generic lattice with two inequivalent singular sublattices, the spectrum contains two alternating families of gaps, and the band edges reproduce the scale-invariant spectra of the associated finite singular system. One gap family closes when the two sublattices become equivalent. In this symmetric limit, however, the primitive period halves, so the apparent band crossing results from Brillouin-zone folding. A different family closes at a critical but genuinely asymmetric configuration. Because the primitive period is preserved, adjacent bands then meet at the center of the original Brillouin zone in a genuine massless Dirac-like cone. Near either critical limit, a small finite gap acts as an effective Dirac mass: the dispersion remains quadratic near a band edge but crosses over to a massive Dirac-like form beyond that narrow region. The band curvatures define effective electron and hole masses. Exact Bloch eigenfunctions further reveal that isospectral lattices can display different single-particle probability densities. This model therefore provides an analytically controlled one-dimensional setting in which conventional quadratic carriers, massive Dirac-like band dispersions, and a tunable massless contact coexist within the same singular lattice.

Permanent Thermodynamic Mpemba Inversion from Quantum-Jump Feedback

We show that a common quantum-jump feedback protocol can permanently reverse the nonequilibrium free-energy ordering of two prescribed initial states while preserving their Gibbs equilibrium. The construction exploits collective compensation between monitored channels: their feedback corrections cancel at equilibrium but remain active during relaxation. This balance preserves free-energy monotonicity while allowing the relaxation modes to be reshaped. We prove that eliminating the complete slow-sector contribution of the initially higher-free-energy state, while retaining it for the comparison state, produces distinct asymptotic decay exponents. Their free-energy ratio then vanishes at long times, guaranteeing a permanent thermodynamic quantum Mpemba inversion under the same controlled dynamics. A thermal qutrit realizes the mechanism through coherence interference and exhibits distinct detection thresholds for spectral selection and exact slow-sector cancellation under feedback retuning.

Multipolar Nanophotonics with Quantum Computers

Small particles respond to light through electric and magnetic patterns called multipoles. Their interference determines the direction of scattered light and affects the decay of nearby emitters. Larger nanoantenna arrays, metasurfaces, and other nanophotonic systems demand increasingly costly simulations to meet strict performance and reliability requirements. We show how to represent these responses with quantum bits (qubits) and use quantum circuits to calculate scattering and the Purcell effect, the change in an emitter's decay rate caused by its surroundings. The circuits can encode known multipoles or estimate unknown ones from Maxwell's equations at the particle surface. We test the method against classical theory using an ideal quantum simulator and real IBM and IonQ quantum computers. For a silicon nanoparticle of radius 78 nm illuminated at 679 nm, IonQ gives a forward directivity of 2.984, close to the ideal dipolar value 3, quantifying the concentration of scattered light in the forward direction. For an emitter 10 nm from a gold nanoparticle and emitting at 510 nm, IBM measurements yield a calculated total decay rate 20.48 times its vacuum value versus 20.47 from theory, with correction for errors in reading the qubit states. We also establish how the number of multipoles and the required accuracy set the demands on qubits and measurements. These results motivate quantum-assisted design studies of directional radiation and emitter decay in nanophotonic systems.

Measuring higher-order bulk topology in a programmable quantum simulator

Topological insulators lie outside the standard Landau paradigm: Instead of being characterized by local order parameters and spontaneous symmetry breaking, their defining properties are encoded in the global structure of their quantum states and manifested as robust boundary states. Crystalline higher-order topological insulators can extend this principle by hosting localized corner modes in two and higher dimensions. Although these boundary modes can be detected spectroscopically through the local density of states, determining the underlying bulk topology, for example through nested Wilson loops, remains an outstanding experimental challenge. Here we use up to 728 qubits in a superconducting analogue-digital quantum simulator to realize and probe a hierarchy of one-, two-, and three-dimensional topological insulators governed by effective tight-binding Hamiltonians. By reconstructing complex propagators from real-space measurements, we obtain complementary boundary and bulk characterizations: local densities of states reveal localized boundary modes, whereas experimentally reconstructed band projectors yield bulk topological invariants, culminating in a three-dimensional octupole moment extracted through third nested Wilson loops. These results establish a route to realizing, characterizing, and manipulating topologically protected states in programmable three-dimensional quantum matter.

Sequential optimization for orbital-optimized variational quantum eigensolvers

Orbital optimization within the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) framework is useful for improving active-space quality and accurately capturing electron correlation with shallow quantum circuits. The orbital-optimized unitary coupled cluster (OO-UCC) method [W. Mizukami et al., Phys. Rev. Res. 2, 033421 (2020)], in its original formulation, employs the orbital gradient and Hessian in a Newton--Laphson procedure. In this study, we propose sequential orbital-optimized unitary coupled cluster doubles (SOO-UCCD), extending the gradient-free sequential minimal optimization approach [K. M. Nakanishi et al., Phys. Rev. Res. 2, 043158 (2020)] to orbital rotation parameters. Based on the frequency structure implied by the algebraic properties of the orbital rotation generators, our method determines each orbital rotation angle from a nine-point energy profile. The energy landscape is evaluated by classically transforming the Hamiltonian integrals and contracting them with the reduced density matrices already measured for the VQE energy evaluation. This allows for orbital optimization without evaluating the orbital gradient or Hessian. By building on the core benefits of the OO-UCC framework, our method provides a gradient-free and hyperparameter-free orbital-optimization procedure. State-vector simulations for LiH in the STO-3G basis show that SOO-UCCD reproduces UCCSD-level accuracy along the bond-stretching curve and exhibits macro-iteration convergence comparable to that of L-BFGS-based orbital optimization.

Entanglement witnesses are a key instrument in determining the entanglement of unknown bipartite qubit systems without requiring a full tomography. Riccardi et al. In this work we propose an additional set of witnesses, denoted $\{W^5\}$, that build on $\{W^3\}$ and require measurements in two additional bases. We present an adaptive two-step method that uses the measurement results obtained for $\{W^3\}$ to select which $W^5$ to evaluate. (2021) while requiring measurements in only five of the nine bases needed for full quantum tomography. Scalable Exact Non-Pauli Noise Simulation for Fault-Tolerant Quantum Circuits

Fault-tolerant quantum design relies on simulating noisy quantum error correction (QEC) circuits with hundreds to thousands of physical qubits and large circuit depths. High-throughput stabilizer simulators, such as Stim, are the de facto tools for this regime. However, these simulators typically support only Clifford gates and stochastic Pauli noise. As a result, more realistic device noise models-such as coherent control errors and thermal relaxation-are often approximated using Pauli Twirling. This replacement offers speed but can introduce inaccuracies.
  To address this, we propose QuasiStim for scalable exact QEC simulation of realistic noise, with QuasiFold as a compiler frontend and DeltaFrame as a sampling backend. QuasiFold lowers sampling overhead by merging equivalent non-Pauli sites in the quantum circuit. DeltaFrame samples the resulting stabilizer trajectories by reusing precomputed predicted references instead of resimulating the full tableau for each shot.
  QuasiFold can reduce the number of shots by over $100\times$ on bivariate-bicycle (BB) codes. Compared to a per-shot tableau simulator, DeltaFrame is $166\times$--$1331\times$ faster on rotated surface-code memory under coherent $R_Z$ noise as $d$ grows from 5 to 35. QuasiStim also makes the PTA bias measurable: the gap is non-negligible and varies with noise strength, decoder, and circuit schedule.
  QuasiStim will be open-sourced upon publication at https://github.com/isaachyw/QuasiStim

Quantum simultaneous decoding via pretty-good measurements of tilted states

A multiple-access channel transmits messages from several senders to one receiver. For quantum outputs, the receiver must distinguish states depending on all messages, while tests for different decoding errors may not commute. In this paper, we give a self-contained account of simultaneous decoding, in which one joint measurement recovers the messages at the established achievable rates using independent codebooks. The proof combines Sen's tilted-subspace method with Cheng's analysis of the pretty-good measurement, a standard measurement for distinguishing quantum states. Auxiliary labels close to a common reference state separate the geometric directions associated with different errors. A Gram-matrix bound then allows separately chosen binary tests to certify the performance of one measurement. The labels are removed from the completed decoder, leading to a deterministic packing theorem that applies to finite state ensembles, including unequal priors. We develop the two-sender case first, with worked examples and figures, before treating any fixed number of senders. Sharper finite-error guarantees for the same decoder are developed separately, with sharp bounds for two specified steps of the analysis rather than a claim of globally optimal decoding. Applications recover known achievable rates for unassisted and entanglement-assisted classical communication, distributed classical compression with quantum side information, and coherent-state communication through a beamsplitter or passive multiport device. The common proof makes the geometric mechanism and its quantitative consequences available without repeating the construction for each application.

Quantum decoherence does not permit the operation of (new) miniaturised devices or protocols that can otherwise run unassisted in classical settings, without explicit user agent intervention in the quantum realm. Classical driving signals are inadequate to model a truly quantum device built entirely in the quantum realm. Local-dimension-invariant low-density parity-check stabilizer codes

The protection of quantum information in systems leveraging more than two basis states (qudits) is an ongoing challenge. A tool for transforming the plethora of qubit stabilizer codes into qudit stabilizer codes is the local-dimension-invariant (LDI) extension to the stabilizer formalism. A drawback of the LDI method is that generally in order to ensure the distance of the resultant code is at least preserved the newly selected local-dimension will need to be any prime bigger than a large cutoff value. In this work we consider the repercussions of this large cutoff value, then by focusing on quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes we are able to bring down this cutoff value. Stabilizer entropy cones as self-dual slices of linear rank cones: a five-party gap and dependence on the local dimension

Entanglement entropies of multipartite pure states satisfy linear inequalities; for stabilizer states, the backbone of quantum error correction, the achievable entropy vectors generate a convex cone. We show that, after a linear lift, the closure of this cone is exactly the self-dual slice of a classical object on one more element than there are parties: the closed cone generated by the rank functions of arrangements of subspaces over $\mathrm{GF}(d)$ for a prime local dimension $d$, and of subgroups of abelian groups of exponent dividing $d$ in general. This correspondence brings two features of rank inequalities into play. First, the purifier acts as an extra variable: already for five parties the stabilizer cone is strictly smaller than the quantum linear rank cone, with which earlier evidence suggested it might coincide; an explicit six-variable inequality witnesses the gap. Second, the characteristic of the field enters, so the cone depends on the local dimension. It does not for up to four parties. With enough parties, the cone of $\mathbb{Z}_d$ qudits is contained in that of $\mathbb{Z}_{d'}$ qudits if and only if $d$ divides $d'$, and already for six parties the logical zero state of Steane's code lies outside the stabilizer cone of every odd local dimension. For this last result we show that the seven-variable characteristic-dependent inequalities we checked all follow from Shannon inequalities on the self-dual slice, and instead transfer one of them through a family of subgroups derived from the Fano plane.

End-to-End Fidelity and Rate Analysis for Zero-Added-Loss Multiplexing

Zero-added-loss multiplexing (ZALM)~[Phys. Rev. Appl. {\bf 19}, 054029 (2023)] is a midpoint-source scheme for entanglement distribution. Its quantum transmitter (QTX) employs two Sagnac sources of polarization-entangled pairs plus partial Bell-state measurements (BSMs) on their idler outputs to herald entanglement in their signal outputs, thus avoiding the switching losses suffered by other multiplexing approaches. When its Sagnac sources' spontaneous parametric downconverters (SPDCs) utilize $χ^{(2)}$ crystals with phase-matched spectral islands, ZALM avoids the tradeoff between heralding efficiency and output-state fidelity incurred when conventional-crystal SPDCs' outputs are channelized by dense wavelength-division multiplexing filters. This paper reports an end-to-end fidelity and rate analysis for islands-based ZALM that includes: SPDC multipair emissions of all orders and partial-BSM losses in the ZALM QTX; loss in propagation from the ZALM QTX to Alice and Bob's quantum receivers (QRXs); loss and mode mismatch in the mode converters employed at Alice and Bob's QRXs; and loss in the partial-BSM loading of Alice and Bob's emissive quantum memories.

Quantum Localization in Geometric Complex Networks

Quantum localization, the phenomenon by which wave diffusion is halted, here understood as the superposition of propagating waves, can have many different origins, such as onsite random potentials in Anderson localization. However, some systems exhibit complex geometries and could potentially reproduce similar results without external potentials. In this work, we study how localization of single particle states is affected by the underlying topology of a simple but highly predictive complex network model: the geometric soft configuration model $\mathbb S^1$. By tuning the model's temperature and heterogeneity parameters, we are able to generate different structural patterns and focus on the transition from a small-world to a non-small-world regime. Our study suggests that small-world networks tend to have extended states, which spread the whole system's size, in contrast to non-small-world networks which tend to localize, showing how complex connectivity can play the role assigned to random energetic disorder.

Eigenstate transitions and critical transport in 3D Aubry-André model

We numerically study spectral and wave-function properties, as well as time evolution, in finite-size commensurate approximations of the 3D Aubry-André model. We explicitly show, through scaling analysis, the existence of two critical points. One of the critical points is a localization-delocalization transition in real-space, similar to the critical point in the 3D Anderson model. The other is a localization-delocalization transition in a dual momentum space, which does not have any analog in the 3D Anderson model. We estimate their values from spectral measures, such as the gap ratio and the spectral form factor, as well as from wave-function measures, such as the inverse participation ratio and the participation entropy. Additionally, we demonstrate that both critical points exhibit hallmarks of critical points between quantum ergodicity and localization. First, the eigenstates and the eigenspectrum at the critical point fulfill the mutual universal relation between the eigenspectrum compressibility $χ$ and the eigenstate fractal dimension $d^{(1)}$ [S. Jiricek et al., Proc. Nat. Acad. Sci. 123, e2518027123 (2026)]. Second, the dynamics of the wave packet at the critical point is governed by the critical dynamical exponent $z$, characterized by the spectral fractal dimension and the dimensionality of the lattice [M. Hopjan and L. Vidmar, Phys. Rev. Lett. 135, 060401 (2025)]. Thus, similarly to the 3D Anderson model, the critical dynamical exponent is sub-diffusive with the value of $z=3$.
---
Quantum ordinal optimization under a fixed comparison budget

Ordinal optimization selects designs that are good enough rather than optimal when evaluation is expensive and the budget is small. The alignment probability measures the chance that the selected set contains at least a prescribed number of truly good designs. We formulate quantum ordinal optimization as a class of problems that maximize this probability under a fixed oracle budget. We study exact, coherent pairwise comparisons with one selected design as a first instance, with the budget counted in comparison queries. For every classical comparison budget smaller than the number of designs, we prove that a knockout among uniformly sampled designs attains the optimum. The sample is drawn without replacement and contains one more design than the number of comparisons. For a uniformly random order, the selected design's rank has a polynomial probability law. At the same comparison budget, the maximum quantum degree is at most twice the classical maximum, a factor attained whenever there are more than twice as many designs as comparisons, so a quantum algorithm can realize rank laws that no classical algorithm with the same budget can. We determine the exact quantum optimum with one comparison for every number of designs and every good-enough set. For selecting the best of n designs, the optimal success probability with one quantum comparison is approximately 3.30/n for large n, compared with 2/n for one classical comparison. When the best design is sought, one quantum comparison gives a higher alignment probability than two classical comparisons whenever there are more than nine designs.
---
Violation of Bell-type Inequality in the Hanbury Brown-Twiss (HBT) Toy-Model: Quantum Correlations beyond Local-Realism

The Hanbury Brown-Twiss (HBT) effect marked a significant milestone in quantum optics by illustrating that intensity correlations can reveal inherently quantum features of light. Motivated by the structure of the original 8-mode HBT interferometer, we develop a simplified 4-mode toy-model that captures its essential physical ingredients while significantly reducing analytical complexity. Our toy-model provides an intuitive and well-structured framework for examining the desired correlation functions. We investigate whether our toy-model can exhibit genuinely non-classical behaviour by formulating and applying a Bell-type inequality tailored to its specific measurement configuration. Our analysis shows that the correlations generated by the toy-model violate the inequality, indicating the presence of non-local features that are incompatible with any classical hidden-variable description. The results highlight the power of simplified models in uncovering fundamental aspects of non-classicality in optical systems.
---
Rate and depth of two-axis twisting by pair exchange under the self-Kerr constraint

In this paper, we study two bosonic modes that exchange pairs of quanta, which realize two-axis twisting exactly: within a sector of fixed particle number the exchange operator is proportional to the two-axis twisting generator, with a fixed factor of two. Because any real nonlinear medium supplies a local Kerr nonlinearity alongside this operator, the two merge into one twisting Hamiltonian governed by a single dimensionless ratio $r=χ_s/χ_e$ of the Kerr strength to the exchange strength. Two conditions then decide how much of the ideal behavior survives this constraint, and they must be met separately. The first concerns speed: the exponential squeezing rate follows in closed form, vanishes exactly at $r=\pm1$, and recovers as the square root of the distance from that point. The second concerns depth: the smallest attainable squeezing parameter depends only on the anisotropy of the mean-field saddle, attaining the full two-axis-twisting value at $r=0$ and $r=\pm3$ regardless of the rate, so that settings sharing the same anisotropy but differing twofold in rate reach the same depth. The two conditions address different targets, and a system may squeeze quickly yet terminate at a shallow optimum; only $r=0$ is optimal in both respects. The closed-form Gaussian solution reveals a further systematic bias: because the early-time decay rate of the variance exceeds twice the exponential rate, a fit to short-time data overestimates the rate by more than a factor of two. Since an isotropic cubic nonlinearity imposes $r=1$, the value at which the rate vanishes, the two strengths must be mismatched, and a moderate mismatch already restores most of the optimal depth. Finally, the constraint is invisible at low excitation: at two quanta the Kerr term shifts both coupled states equally, so the two Hamiltonians are indistinguishable until four quanta.
