An optimization problem for $δ'$ leaky stars

We consider singular Schrödinger operators with an attractive $δ'$ interaction of strength $β$ supported by a star graph and prove that the ground-state eigenvalue is uniquely maximized by stars of the maximum symmetry. Two different proofs are presented, one for an even number of edges using a comparison to the $δ$ interaction star, the other general based on Birman-Schwinger technique.
---
Nonperturbative optomechanics with thin, weakly reflective intracavity dielectrics

Levitated dielectric sensors are often treated as perturbative probes of a pre-existing optical field. We show that this picture fails for a novel sensor geometry: a dielectric plate whose transverse extent captures the full optical mode. Even an optically thin sensor with modest refractive index can heavily restructure the intracavity fields, effectively partitioning a cavity into two subcavities with substantial cross-coupling and producing new optical resonances and trapping sites with properties which deviate from the standard intuition. Conventional perturbative expressions for the cavity frequency shift and optomechanical coupling depart from the exact solution at thicknesses as small as $\simλ/(10n)$. Furthermore, cavity locking decreases the coupling and increases the trap stiffness by orders of magnitude, and the coupled subcavity structure permits appreciable optical cooling at zero detuning, a distinct behavior unique to this nonperturbative regime. These effects, sharing a common origin, establish a distinct regime of cavity optomechanics in which the sensor is no longer merely a probe of the cavity field, but an element that helps define it.
---
Variational Minimization of Almost Strong Zero Modes in Parafermionic Wires

Strongly interacting fermionic chains obeying a $Z_n$ symmetry can host topological phases with highly degenerate ground-state manifolds. The different ground states are connected by strongly bound fractionalized edge modes called parafermionic zero modes. While exactly preserved under time evolution at zero temperature, interactions lead to a destabilization of these modes at finite energy densities, and, given a generic Hamiltonian, obtaining the lifetime of these modes is often impossible as it requires knowledge of the full spectrum of the Hamiltonian or long time evolutions. Here, we propose a novel quantum-inspired algorithm that can be used to variationally search for these almost strong zero modes in generic parafermionic chain models. We demonstrate analytically and numerically that our ansatz faithfully reproduces the exact results and finds strongly bound parafermionic operators in the non-integrable Kitaev-Hubbard chain and chiral Potts model. Our results further allow us to verify the existence of a true strong zero mode in the symmetrical limit of the chiral Potts model for $Z_3$-parafermions.
---
Spatial interference changes the symmetry class of a Josephson element

Spatial interference can break the inversion symmetry of an effective Josephson potential even in a laterally homogeneous junction with inversion-symmetric local energy. We derive a general criterion based on harmonic-dependent complex spatial form factors: a single local harmonic remains a shifted cosine, whereas multiple harmonics can acquire phases incompatible with any common inversion center, permitting cubic interactions and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order expansion coefficients about corresponding minima while preserving the even-order ones. For a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge, it also preserves the complete quantum spectrum, enabling reversal of the odd nonlinear sector without spectral retuning. A centered cross junction with multiple local harmonics is one concrete example. Within the imposed-field model, a three-component magnetic field can generate an inversion-asymmetric effective potential, and reversing an odd number of field components exactly reflects it without changing the quantum spectrum of the capacitively shunted junction.
---
Molecular Vibronic Complexity as a Quantum Resource for Optimal Control: Nonadiabatic Mixing Creates Protected Coherence Subspaces in Dissipative Environments

In molecules, electronic coherence typically decays within a few hundred femtoseconds, a timescale usually attributed to the environment. We show that it is set to a large extent by which coherences are excited: the molecule can host coherences that the bath barely sees, and optimal control can find and exploit them. Using gradient-based optimal control (GRAPE) with Redfield dynamics, we maximize the total vibronic coherence between the electronic ground and an optically bright electronic excited state in a three-state model with nonadiabatic features, in two regimes of low and high nonadiabatic mixing and for different system-bath coupling strengths. The Liouvillian spectrum reveals, in both regimes, a well-separated coherence mode with substantial ground-bright character that decays on a picosecond timescale. This mode is protected by mixing with the dark state, which brings the geometry of the optically accessible excited state close to that of the ground state and thus weakens its coupling to the bath. Without prior information, GRAPE finds this mode and keeps the photoexcited wave packet near the Franck-Condon region. At equal fluence, it increases the coherence at 800 fs by more than two orders of magnitude over a Gaussian pulse at the weakest coupling, and by many more at stronger coupling. The Liouvillian predicts the bath dependence of the long-lived coherence to within 1\%, and a Gaussian pulse tuned to the mode frequency comes within 10\% of the best pulse found. Beyond two-level and spin systems, our study puts forward the vibronic structure of molecules as a route to protected coherence subspaces, identifying a dark-state protection mechanism and a design criterion based on molecular parameters. These results lay the groundwork for our goal of using vibronic molecular systems as a resource for long-lived, controllable coherences in molecular quantum information science.
---
Separability criteria and lower bounds of entanglement via realignment

The detection and quantification of quantum entanglement are central problems in quantum information science. Based on the realignment of density matrices and the vectorization of reduced density matrices, we construct a family of enhanced separability criteria for bipartite and multipartite quantum systems. Our criteria recover the previously proposed realignment-based criteria as special cases and detect more entanglement than the earlier ones. Moreover, we derive tighter lower bounds for the concurrence and the convex-roof extended negativity, criteria for detecting the genuine tripartite entanglement, and lower bounds on genuine-multipartite-entanglement concurrence. The advantages of our results are demonstrated with detailed examples.
---
Non-Stationary Quantum Systems Associated with Stationary Scattering in One Dimension

Stationary scattering admits a dynamical formulation in which the scattering data are extracted from the evolution operator for an effective non-stationary and non-unitary quantum system. This provides a mathematical method of solving scattering problems. We consider the possibility of realizing such a quantum system and relating the scattering information to outcomes of quantum measurements performed on it. To this end, we examine stationary scattering problems in one dimension where the scattering data consist of reflection and transmission amplitudes and the corresponding effective quantum system is a non-unitary two-level system. The states of this system identify points in a Bloch sphere $S^2$ and their evolution yields trajectories in $S^2$. Quantum measurements turn out to be capable of providing information about the reflection amplitudes. We identify configurations displaying reflectionlessness with situations where one or both of the poles of $S^2$ undergo cyclic evolutions. Spectral singularities and coherent perfect absorption correspond to dynamical spin flips which map one of the poles of $S^2$ to the other.

Proposal for a multi-ion optical clock with hundreds of ions

Scaling trapped-ion optical clocks to hundreds of ions remains a major challenge due to inhomogeneous frequency shifts in large ion crystals. Here, we propose a circular surface-trap architecture for a multi-ion optical clock with hundreds of ions. The rotational symmetry of the ion crystal suppresses the main sources of inhomogeneity, quadrupole shift.We investigate the feasibility of a clock based on a ring crystal of 400 $^{40}\mathrm{Ca}^{+}$ ions and propose a suitable experimental setup configuration. We have shown that circular configuration of ions leads to suppression by two orders of magnitude of the quadrupole shift comparing to the linear trap. Combined with stray-field compensation and dynamical decoupling techniques, this architecture provides a pathway toward multi-ion optical clocks with fractional frequency uncertainty at the $10^{-18}$ level and an expected 20-fold improvement in frequency stability compared to single ion clocks.
---
A Quantum Analogue of the Covering Lemma: Limits of Exact Anonymous Computation

Can quantum communication determine the size of an anonymous network when no size bound is known? Classical impossibility proofs rely on the covering lemma: an execution can be reproduced on a larger network with the same local structure. Quantum interference invalidates this argument - a measurement record possible on a network can become impossible on a cover. We show that the impossibility of exact counting nevertheless survives. Our main result is a quantum analogue of the covering lemma that preserves local possibilities rather than complete executions. Within a cyclic covering family, every local measurement outcome possible at a node remains possible at a corresponding node in every sufficiently large network. This holds even after information has travelled around the smaller network, beyond the reach of a simple locality argument. We extend the principle to the universal covering tree and finite covers with sufficiently long cycles. The proof combines tensor-network methods with graph coverings: a common representation across network sizes turns normalization into a constraint on which local outcomes can occur. For uniform synchronous protocols with independently prepared nodes, this support-lifting principle rules out zero-error counting and even the production of a guaranteed finite size bound on anonymous rings and the other cyclic covering families considered here. It also rules out exact leader election with termination in finite time with probability one on these families. Measurements, classical control, and local memory growing with time are allowed; no predetermined running-time bound is assumed. Quantum interference thus defeats execution lifting without enabling infallible certification of network size.
---
Riemannian Formulation of Sample-Based Quantum Krylov Solvers for Elliptic Problems: Energy-Norm Guarantees and Duct-Flow Applications

Sample-based quantum linear solvers measure quantum Krylov states $e^{-iAT}|b\rangle$ and reconstruct the solution of $Ax=b$ classically on the observed basis states. We develop this approach for positive-definite systems that arise from elliptic partial differential equations. We show that the sampling guarantees hold in any fixed basis with an efficient transform. We then replace the variational reconstruction by a Galerkin solve on the sampled set. For this reconstruction we derive expected and high-probability energy-norm error bounds, a sample complexity in terms of the smallest sampling probability on an essential set, a computable residual certificate, and a support-completeness theorem for the dephased outcome law. We formulate the method for elliptic problems on Riemannian cross sections. In two dimensions the stiffness matrix is metric-independent in conformal coordinates, and curved vessels lead to the same matrix class. Killing symmetries of the cross section give selection rules for the measurement outcomes, and the Lie derivative of the metric gives first-order bounds for geometric perturbations. We separate operator approximations, whose effect we bound, from sampler approximations, which affect coverage but not the target. As examples we treat the implicit step of pulsatile flow in elliptic, lens-shaped, and curved ducts. In simulations of up to 12 qubits, the number of Fourier modes required for 1% error grows from 14 to 60 while the number of unknowns grows from 64 to 4096. A classical residual-greedy selection is nevertheless more efficient than quantum sampling in all cases studied, consistent with the low measured sampling efficiency. On a six-qubit trapped-ion processor, the measured outcome laws follow a replacement-noise model whose strength grows by about 0.6% per two-qubit gate, and a parity selection rule rejects shots at the predicted rate.
---
Ergodicity breaking transition beyond qubits

The local Hilbert space dimension is a fundamental parameter in quantum many-body systems, but its role in controlling the ergodicity-breaking transition remains less explored. We systematically study this question by introducing toy models of qudits in zero dimensions that exhibit an ergodicity-breaking transition. Using the hybridization condition, we analytically obtain the dependence of the critical value of the interactions on the local Hilbert space dimension. Numerical studies of spectral correlation measures show agreement with the theoretical predictions, hence confirming the existence of the ergodicity-breaking transition in these models. We further introduce a distinct higher spin version of the model and numerically obtain the same critical behavior, demonstrating that the observed critical point is independent of the microscopic structure of interactions considered.
---
Breakdown of thermalization in quantum spin systems with long-range interactions

Quantum thermalization of closed systems -- i.e. the relaxation of local properties to their thermodynamic value along the Hamiltonian evolution -- is generically expected in non-integrable systems at finite energy density. In this work we show that quantum spin systems with at least one spin component coupled through long-range ferromagnetic interactions, and a second spin component coupled to a field, cannot in general thermalize to a paramagnetic state whenever they have a finite, negative energy per spin. The long-range interactions we consider decay as a power law of the distance, $\sim r^{-α}$ with $α< d$, where $d$ is the number of spatial dimensions of the system. The lack of thermalization is generic to an infinite family of Hamiltonians, and it is not associated to any form of integrability. It can instead be understood by the anomalous features of the thermodynamics of the system in the paramagnetic phase, which cannot be reflected in the properties of the eigenstates. This leads to a breakdown of eigenstate typicality and of the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis. Interestingly, the non-thermal states emerging at long times in the dynamics display long-range multipartite entanglement, elevating the breakdown of thermalization to a resource.
---
Quantum circuit representation and incomplete Bell measurements in Compton scattering

We formulate massless tree-level Compton scattering as an effective two-qubit operation on electron and photon helicities. In the Bell basis, the scattering map separates into two parity sectors and admits a simple quantum circuit representation with one nonunitary filter. After Kraus normalization, this filter defines an unsharp incomplete Bell measurement that distinguishes the two Bell-parity sectors but not the relative Bell label within each sector. We further show that the incomplete measurement alone does not surpass the classical teleportation threshold, whereas a subsequent complete Bell-basis readout enables unit conditional teleportation fidelity.
---
Genuine Information Backflow Requires Noncommuting Initial System States

The Gorini-Kossakowski-Lindblad-Sudarshan (GKLS) equation, a cornerstone of open-system theory, describes Markovian semigroup dynamics in which information about the initial preparation cannot revive. Beyond this regime, information revivals are widely interpreted as backflow from a non-Markovian environment. However, a decrease followed by an increase in information retrievable from the system need not imply that information has returned from the environment [PRX Quantum 6, 020316 (2025)]. It may instead arise from noise correlations unrelated to the system's initial preparation, carried by inert degrees of freedom that never interact with the system and therefore cannot mediate backflow. Such revivals are called extrinsic; genuine backflow is reserved for revivals that cannot be explained this way. The framework developed in that work, however, assumes access to arbitrary initial system states, an assumption often incompatible with physical constraints or limited experimental control. This leaves open which properties of the available preparations are necessary for an information revival to certify genuine backflow. Here we extend the framework to allow the description of restricted families of initial states and show that genuine backflow is possible only if the family contains noncommuting states. If all available states commute, as in a classical stochastic process, every revival can be explained by a suitable inert extension and is therefore extrinsic. Noncommutativity of the available system states is thus necessary for information revivals to provide an operational signature of environmental memory.
---
Criticality Concentrates Extensive Sensing Information into a Local Readout

We show that criticality concentrates extensive sensing information into a local trajectory. In the rapid-readout limit of an all-to-all dissipative feedback network, a single binary trajectory has a Fisher-information rate linear in $N$ for a common weak field, as does the full record. Critical slowing does not remove this gain because the local weight of the slow mode decreases with $N$. An analytic bound guarantees that the local record retains more than half of the full information across the entire channel-parameter range. We also identify how parameter drives and observation dynamics determine local access to collective information beyond permutation symmetry. Furthermore, our sparse-readout theorem establishes a $k^{-1}$ decay of fractional information loss: only $Θ(\varepsilon^{-1})$ trajectories are needed to retain a fraction $1-\varepsilon$ of the full information rate. Any growing readout set, even of vanishing density, asymptotically recovers the full common-field Fisher-information rate.

Receiver-Constrained Recovery Placement for Encoded-Qutrit Teleportation under Monitored Decay

Using established probabilistic measurement-reversal theory, we study recovery placement in encoded-qutrit teleportation under monitored decay. We characterize the success advantage of reversing a no-jump map only on the encoded support and compare two receiver capabilities. The restricted-map construction applies to general finite-dimensional supports; explicit timing results are obtained for a coherent V-type sender, a $Λ$-type receiver, and $S=\{0,2\}$, within a prescribed family of maximal exact sender reversals. With one outcome-independent full-space receiver filter, we maximize success probability at fixed total time subject to a minimum success-weighted conditional fidelity. A single preassigned recovery time yields up to three duration-dependent regimes: no sender reversal, the earliest feasible intermediate reversal, and final-time reversal. If recovery times may be randomized independently of the input and only the pooled successful ensemble must meet the fidelity requirement, the optimum over all such timing distributions mixes the two endpoints for $ΓT \ge (\ln 3)/2$. With Bell-outcome-dependent nonunitary receiver recovery, each accepted full-column-rank transfer branch can instead be reversed exactly. For the same model and sender-operation family, omitting sender reversal uniquely maximizes the exact-teleportation success probability for every finite $T>0$ at positive decay rate. Partial pathway coherence further distinguishes relative support gain from absolute recovery success: the long-time dark-state plateau survives only for fully coherent decay. These results show how receiver control capabilities and the meaning of the fidelity constraint determine whether intermediate sender recovery is useful.
---
Photonic realization of an electromagnetic cavity field with uniform mirror motion

We perform a detailed mathematical study of the effective Hamiltonian describing a non-stationary electromagnetic cavity field, introduced by C.~K.~Law, in the presence of uniform linear motion. We find that the corresponding dynamical equations admit a unitary diagonalization and that the normalized velocity of motion satisfies the two-sided bound $\lvert v/c \rvert \lesssim 10.1716$. Although this result is mathematically consistent, the interval obtained is an order of magnitude larger than the physically admissible range $\lvert v/c \rvert < 1$. To address this limitation, we propose a reinterpretation of bosonic coupling terms as generators of the symplectic algebra $\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})$, identifying them with transformations that can be implemented through linear optical elements. Within this photonic approach, the coupling parameters are not restricted to small values, allowing us to explore regimes of strong effective interactions. In this case, we demonstrate the generation of two-mode squeezed states and derive analytical expressions for the average photon number in each mode. Furthermore, we analyze statistical properties that characterize mixedness and entanglement, including intermode correlations, the Mandel parameter, and the linear entropy. The corresponding Wigner function provides a phase-space visualization of the system's behavior, illustrating the connection between cavity motion, mode coupling, and nonclassical field correlations via the underlying symplectic algebra.

Rydberg Atomic Quantum Receivers for OFDM Systems: An Atomic-Comb Design

Rydberg atomic quantum receivers (RAQR) exhibit exceptional sensitivity to radio-frequency (RF) signals, positioning them as promising candidates for next-generation wireless systems. Extending their capabilities to orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM), however, requires atomic responses that go beyond broadband reception and are tailored to the structured interference of multicarrier signals. To bridge this gap, we develop an atomic-comb Rydberg receiver based on six-wave mixing (SWM) transduction. In particular, injecting a multi-tone local oscillator (LO) into the RAQR generates frequency-shifted replicas of the intrinsic SWM response. By subtly tuning the amplitude and phase of each LO tone, constructive and destructive interference can be selectively induced to preserve desired response components while attenuating harmful frequency offsets. Furthermore, we harness the response-reshaping capability of the proposed atomic comb to proactively mitigate OFDM interference. More explicitly, by exploiting the discrete difference-frequency structure of both carrier-frequency-offset (CFO)-induced and phase-noise (PN)-induced inter-carrier interference (ICI), we optimize the atomic-comb response to suppress dominant ICI components directly. We further reveal an intrinsic coupling between spectral shaping and the time-domain effective channel memory, motivating a joint optimization of both ICI and inter-symbol interference (ISI), which simultaneously suppresses inter-carrier leakage and post-cyclic-prefix (CP) tail energy. Extensive simulation results validate our atomic-comb design, unveiling that the proposed atomic comb can effectively reshape the receiver response over the OFDM, while mitigating ICI, shortening the effective channel memory, and substantially lowering CP overhead.
---
Primordial Graviton Entanglement in Quasicrystalline Inflation

We study the entanglement structure of gravitons in quasicrystalline inflation. Both parity-even and parity-odd interactions generate graviton pairs with nonzero total momentum. For opposite-helicity pairs with identical momenta, the parity-even contribution can be nonzero, whereas the parity-odd contribution vanishes. The transition from inflation to radiation domination produces additional conventional two-mode squeezing. We derive the resulting normal and anomalous covariances. The initially nonzero reciprocal blocks of the full field-momentum covariance remain nonzero under homogeneous evolution, although individual components can vanish. We show that this homogeneous, helicity-preserving evolution acts unitarily within each helicity sector and therefore preserves the entanglement between the complete right- and left-helicity sectors. Nevertheless, it can eliminate entanglement in the reduced state of a co-propagating pair. We illustrate this distinction using a solvable four-mode model.

Monogamy of quantum steering in exciton--cavity optomechanical system

In this work, we study a theoretical scheme to investigate the monogamy of quantum steering in a hybrid exciton--cavity optomechanical (EOM) system. Within the Gaussian-state formalism, we quantify the steerability among the exciton, photon, and mechanical modes in both the steady-state and dynamical regimes. We establish and verify CKW-type monogamy inequalities for Gaussian steering and demonstrate that the distribution of bipartite steering among the three modes can be controlled through the system parameters. We further examine the influence of thermal noise and temporal evolution on the steering properties and the corresponding monogamy relations. Our results show that increasing the temperature generally suppresses the strength of Gaussian steering and modifies the degree of steering sharing imposed by the CKW-type monogamy relations. In contrast, in the dynamical regime, the steerability can increase with evolution time over suitable temporal windows, indicating that the transient dynamics can be exploited to enhance quantum correlations. These results highlight the potential of hybrid exciton--optomechanical platforms for controllable quantum correlations and provide a promising route toward the generation and manipulation of multipartite quantum resources.
---
Signal-to-noise problems in fault-tolerant quantum computation

Quantum computation offers a promising alternative to classical Monte Carlo methods by naturally avoiding sign problems through unitary evolution. A common misconception follows from this: because quantum simulation sidesteps the sign problem, it is often assumed to be an essentially perfect substitute for classical sampling. This is false---quantum measurements are intrinsically stochastic, and estimating observables often requires a number of samples that grows exponentially with system size, time separation, or inverse temperature, exactly as in the classical signal-to-noise problem. In this work, we analyze the origin and structure of signal-to-noise problems in quantum computation, drawing parallels with classical Monte Carlo, and demonstrate that such noise persists even in sign-problem-free regimes. We discuss strategies to mitigate it, in particular a quantum generalization of Stein control variates construction, in which unbiased estimators are built from operator commutators $[X,A]$ and their discrete diagonal counterparts, and validate the approach with exactly solvable numerical examples that quantify the achievable variance reduction. Our results underscore that variance reduction, not merely sign-problem avoidance, is required to make quantum algorithms practically viable for many-body physics.
---
The Rényi area law conjecture is false below $α=1$ in high dimensions

Area laws express the expectation that entanglement in gapped quantum ground states scales with a boundary rather than a volume. In one dimension this holds for every Rényi entropy, and the Rényi area-law conjecture asserts the same in higher dimensions for each fixed $0<α<1$. Here we disprove this conjecture in every spatial dimension two and higher. We construct finite-range Hamiltonians with fixed local dimension, bounded interaction strength, unique ground states and a uniform spectral gap, whose ground states exhibit volume-law Rényi entanglement while obeying a von Neumann area law and remaining connected to product states through uniformly gapped local paths. The mechanism is error-space complexity: locally identifiable components of arbitrarily small weight can encode states with high entanglement complexity. We also establish the worst-case rank-error trade-off for approximate ground-state projectors, a key tool in one-dimensional area-law proofs. At fixed gap, the optimal Schmidt rank at projection error $ε$ across a planar cut of cross-section $A$ scales as $\exp[\tildeΘ((A\logε^{-1})^{2/3})]$ when $\logε^{-1}\gtrsim A$. Thus $α=1$ is the sharp threshold for a universal Rényi area law within this adiabatically connected class.
---
Generative Quantum Machine Learning Methods for Cancer Detection using Augmented Datasets

Cancer detection using AI methods and image databases faces several challenges, including limited and imbalanced image datasets, long algorithm training times, and high-complexity feature generation. In particular, many cancer image datasets contain very few malignant examples compared to benign ones, limiting the amount of minority-class data available for training. Although classical AI and especially deep learning methods have had some success in providing cancer diagnostics, Quantum Machine Learning (QML) methods may offer advantages through high-dimensional feature representations, generative data augmentation, and the exploitation of feature correlations through quantum properties such as entanglement. In this paper, we propose using a generative QML method on the compressed breast ultrasound image dataset (BUSI) to generate synthetic images and hence augmented datasets for classifier training. In particular, we train a convolutional neural network with real, synthetic, and a combination of real and synthetic images. We test the algorithm on real images and provide comparative results for all three training cases. Our results show that the classifier trained entirely on synthetic images achieved an AUC-ROC of 0.802 (80.2%), compared with 0.760 (76.0%) for the real-data baseline.
---
Generative learning of time series with holographic quantum circuit sampling

Quantum generative models face growing qubit requirements and concentrated loss landscapes when learning long time series. Here we introduce holographic quantum circuit sampling (HQCS), which combines mid-circuit measurements, qubit reuse and independently parameterized temporal blocks to generate trajectories with a fixed number of qubits. For Haar-random HQCS and a normalized exponential kernel, we prove inverse-polynomial bounds on maximum mean discrepancy loss variance, contrasting with exponential concentration in global Haar-random generators. For generic members of a family of nonstationary Markov processes, temporal parameterization reduces exact-generation gate counts from quadratic to linear in sequence length relative to a specified class of repeated shared-block generators. Simulations demonstrate learning of Gaussian autoregressive processes and historical Volatility Index (VIX) trajectories, with the latter achieving over tenfold lower maximum mean discrepancy than the tested classical benchmarks. These results establish a framework for quantum time-series generation with fixed qubit requirements and provable avoidance of exponential loss concentration as sequence length grows.
---
Entanglement detection in dynamical quantum systems

Quantum science and engineering increasingly target the exploitation and control of large-scale dynamical systems. As system size grows, novel phenomena and quantum advantages emerge, but the resulting many-body dynamics become correspondingly harder to monitor and characterize. In particular, the underlying Hamiltonian often lacks symmetries, and the induced many-body dynamics quickly become hard to track. Focusing on one of the most important quantum features---entanglement---we demonstrate that, for a broad class of common Hamiltonian models, one can design effective witnesses that detect both the presence and structure of entanglement over relatively long evolution times. Inspired by stabilizer-based simulation, we track the evolution of generalized stabilizers of the underlying state and use them to construct linear entanglement witnesses. Through numerical experiments on Heisenberg-type Hamiltonians with up to twelve qubits, we show that the required number of measurement settings can be kept at a modest scale as the system size increases, and entanglement detection remains effective over evolution times that significantly exceed those accessible to naive approaches. The corresponding calculations can be carried out largely symbolically. Furthermore, unlike accurate state simulation, the stabilizer-based design of entanglement witnesses can be performed in a much more coarse-grained, digitized manner. We hope these results will stimulate further exploration of the quantum control of large systems.

Partial-coherence weak measurement of polarization qubits with a temporal pointer: theory and experiment

Weak measurement has proven useful for metrology, for probing quantum paradoxes, and for the direct measurement of quantum states. Weak measurements of polarization qubits with temporal pointers provide a direct route to weak-value characterization, including in regimes where the pointer is only partially coherent. We derive a closed-form expression for the post-selected temporal shift that interpolates between the incoherent and coherent (ideal) weak-measurement limits through a single parameter $ν$, the pointer visibility. The expression is experimentally tested for a range of polarization states spanning three trajectories corresponding to rotations about the principal axes of the Poincaré sphere. Two of the trajectories provide parameter-free benchmarks, with the real part of the weak value following $\cos^2θ$ and $1/2$. In contrast, the weak value along the third trajectory is sensitive to incoherence and is well described by the single-parameter partial-coherence model, from which a reduced pointer visibility is obtained. Time shifts are extracted from the smoothed derivative of the pulse leading edge with internal calibration, reaching few-picosecond precision on 10 ns optical pulses using commercial components. The same apparatus also gives qualitative access to the imaginary quadrature through the frequency centroid, showing that temporal weak measurements remain accessible and quantitative in partially coherent pulsed systems.

A complete classification of operator controllability for closed three-level quantum systems driven by a single control

In this work, we study operator controllability of finite-dimensional closed quantum systems driven by a single coherent control. Two general Lie-algebraic results on controllability of such systems are proved. The first gives sufficient conditions under which controllability of the system is preserved when controlled energy-level shifts are added. The second gives a sufficient basis-element condition under which the dynamical Lie algebra contains $\mathfrak{su}(N)$ and the system is therefore operator controllable. These results, together with known controllability conditions, are applied to obtain a complete classification of operator controllability of all three-level quantum systems in terms of their free and interaction Hamiltonian matrix elements. This classification covers all combinations of energy-level and level-spacing degeneracies, two or three allowed transitions, as well as the presence or absence of controlled energy-level shifts, and gives necessary and sufficient conditions for operator controllability in each case.

Spiral Edge States in Topological 2D Sb2Te3 Crystals

This work explores the edge state conduction properties of twinned and spiraled, 2D Sb2Te3 topological insulator crystals. We show the structure supports spatially continuous edge states within two spiral and knotted edge conduction channels. The channels are connected through a topological defect. This geometry introduces boundary conditions into the 2D crystal that modifies the edge state dispersion. Using a combination of low loss Electron Energy Loss Spectroscopy, Magnetic Force Microscopy and traditional carrier transport measurements we identify a distinct excitation at 0.155 eV in the spiral that seems to be related to two isolated states within the gap. This state is localized to the crystal edges. The modification of edge state bands using manipulation of a systems quantum geometry may open new avenues for the use of 2D topological systems.

Entanglement generation from degeneracy breaking of superradiant Liouvillian steady states by local operations

Superradiance is a collective optical effect where emitters cooperatively radiate with a maximal intensity scaling proportional to the square of the number of emitters. Despite the superradiant cascade transitioning through highly entangled states, it has been shown that, for dynamics along the Dicke ladder, superradiance need not generate entanglement between the emitters. By contrast, we show how introducing incoherent local processes -- normally considered detrimental to both entanglement and superradiance -- can generate high levels of inter-emitter entanglement. We develop a general framework, using degenerate non-Hermitian perturbation theory, for the study of superradiant Liouvillian steady-state degeneracy breaking by these local operations from a few emitters up into the thermodynamic limit. From this, we are able to show that entanglement becomes ubiquitous in this regime and demonstrate its robustness against other forms of symmetric environmental noise. In the regime where pumping dominates, we find that population accumulates towards the maximally entangled minimal pseudo-spin states, corresponding to multi-emitter singlet states. This provides a deeper insight into many-body entanglement and the curious connection between local operations and entanglement generation.

Classical Communication Helps Single-Shot Quantum Compression

Free classical communication can yield an unbounded single-shot saving in quantum compression, even though it leaves the asymptotic rate of an i.i.d. source unchanged. At every fixed error in $(0,1)$, we exhibit sources whose unassisted compression cost is arbitrarily large, yet a single qubit suffices with classical communication and no preshared entanglement. We establish this separation by determining the exact optimal source fidelity for compression through an $m$-dimensional noiseless quantum channel, with and without free classical communication, while preserving correlations with an inaccessible reference. Without classical assistance, the optimal fidelity is exactly the Ky Fan norm $\|ρ\|_{(m)}$, the sum of the $m$ largest eigenvalues of the source state. With classical assistance, we obtain an explicit spectral threshold formula and a protocol attaining it. Classical communication strictly improves fidelity whenever $m$ is smaller than the source rank. The corresponding compression costs are two smoothed versions of the log-rank entropy, also called max-entropy: trace-distance smoothing under local operations and quadratic smoothing under LOCC. For i.i.d. copies of a fixed source with positive entropy variance, however, the two costs have identical first-, second-, and third-order asymptotic terms. For nonlattice spectra, we determine the constant-order advantage: classical communication saves $\log_2(e)-1$ bits per block asymptotically, and the ratio of assisted to unassisted minimum communication dimensions converges to $2/e$.

Avoiding Defects within Heavy-Hexagon Architecture

When implementing scalable fault-tolerant quantum computing using superconducting quantum processors, one of the critical challenges to overcome is the presence of defective qubits arising from fabrication and calibration imperfections. A defect on a specific qubit severely restricts the connectivity between adjacent qubits, disrupts stabilizer measurements, induces frequent detection events, and consequently inflates the logical error rate. To mitigate this issue, various strategies have been proposed, such as syndrome extraction circuits based on stabilizer folding and the construction of super-stabilizers via gauge operators. However, these methods have primarily been developed for architectures with relatively high connectivity of 3 to 4. Furthermore, experimental demonstrations showing an actual reduction in logical error rates in the presence of physical hardware defects have been lacking. In this work, we propose a defect-avoidance scheme tailored for the heavy-hexagon architecture, which features a lower connectivity of 2 to 3. While previous approaches required four measurement cycles to measure all gauge operators comprising a super-stabilizer, our proposed scheme completes all gauge measurements within only two cycles. We experimentally validate this approach on IBM quantum hardware by identifying physical defects and implementing the defect-avoidance circuits. As a result, avoiding the defect yields an approximately 10\% reduction in the logical error rate per cycle for both logical $X$ and $Z$ errors. These findings demonstrate that defects in superconducting hardware can be successfully handled even under the constrained connectivity of heavy-hexagon lattices, and that defect avoidance substantially reduces logical errors on real quantum hardware.

Clifford Invariants and Three-Copy Quantum Tests

We give an explicit treatment of the third tensor power of the Clifford representation on $n$ qutrits, for $n\geq2$. This representation has five pairwise inequivalent irreducible constituents, with multiplicities $1,1,1,1,2$. The decomposition reduces the Clifford twirl of a tensor cube to four real parameters. Two of them are spectral moments of the input. The other two are cubic invariants expressed as sums of cubes of the Weyl characteristic function and the discrete Wigner function. On trace-one Hermitian matrices, the invariant polynomials of degree at most three are spanned by the constant function and four algebraically independent functions. These functions do not separate Clifford orbits. For pure inputs a single parameter suffices. We recover its range and the characterization of its maximum by stabilizer states. We bound the stabilizer fidelity, determine full separability across the three copies in the symmetric family, and recover the criterion for a complex projective $3$-design. We classify the jointly invariant quantum Boolean functions. For stabilizer testing and for testing unknown observables we give explicit bounds on copies and queries. We also give character formulas for the finite projective Clifford groups and record the complete character table for two qutrits.

Reducing Trust Cost in Quantum Application Development Using AI

Generative AI can be a powerful tool for supporting the design and implementation of quantum applications, whereas the burden of checking whether generated circuits realize the intended computation increases. In this study, we regard this burden of human understanding, checking, and judgment as ``trust cost,'' and propose a development methodology that integrates AI and formal verification to reduce it. In the proposed development methodology, the implementation is divided into three components: a target mathematical specification, a quantum circuit, and a correspondence theorem and its proof that prove the agreement between the two. This division separates the specifications and theorem statements checked by humans from the theorem proofs entrusted to mechanical checking. As a concrete development example of this proposal, we describe an implementation for the time evolution of a stochastic particle system and its results.

Scalability and performance metrics for boson sampling devices

Linear-optical platforms provide a promising route to demonstrate quantum computational advantage, particularly through the boson sampling problem. While a boson sampling system should be large enough to show the quantum advantage, the size of the boson sampling problem is often restricted by experimental constraints. In particular, as a boson sampling system is scaled up, experimental constraints can limit its effective problem size to permit efficient classical simulation. In this paper, we investigate the theoretical scalability bounds for boson sampling systems under practical experimental conditions, including photon loss, mode mismatch, and input state error. We employ non-negativity conditions for phase-space quasiprobability distribution functions to analyze these effects. In addition, we introduce two device-level metrics, the boson sampling score which measures the usable capacity of a given device and the boson sampling volume which provides a common hardware-level benchmark for comparing different platforms and assessing their access to an advantage-relevant problem size regime. Our results provide fundamental insights into the performance of boson sampling systems under realistic experimental conditions and offer practical guidelines for scaling up boson sampling systems.

Exploring Dynamical Quantum Phase Transitions via the Dynamical Phase Accumulated at an Equilibrium Critical Point

We investigate dynamical quantum phase transitions (DQPTs) generated by a two-step linear quench with a finite waiting time at the equilibrium critical point. The waiting stage leads to the accumulation of a dynamical phase that depends on the waiting time and directly reflects the critical spectral structure. In the transverse Ising chain, this accumulated phase modifies the critical structure of DQPTs from a single-critical-mode (SCM) regime to a multiple-critical-mode (MCM) regime. We analytically and numerically determine the boundary between the two regimes. Along this boundary, the waiting time approaches a constant in the sudden-quench limit, whereas in the slow-quench limit it follows Kibble--Zurek (KZ) scaling with the quench time. In the SCM regime, the rate function exhibits a conventional linear singularity, $r(t)-r(t^{*})\propto |t-t^{*}|^α$, with the critical exponent $α=1$ near the critical time. At the SCM--MCM boundary, this exponent changes to $α=1/2$, leading to a divergent first derivative of the rate function. Additional discrete parameter lines with $α=1/2$ also appear within the MCM regime in the parameter plane spanned by the waiting and quench times. Our findings establish a direct connection between the accumulated dynamical phase and the critical structure of DQPTs, further supported by an extended Su--Schrieffer--Heeger (SSH) model.

Entanglement-Capability-Aware Finite-Bond Circuit Cutting for Distributed Quantum Computing

Distributed quantum computing distributes workloads that exceed a single processor's capacity across resource-constrained components. Circuit cutting enables this decomposition, but its effectiveness depends on partition choice. We present SACE, a Simulation-Aware Circuit Partitioning framework with Entanglement-Capability-Aware Screening. EP-Cost screens partitions using a parameter-dependent entanglement-capability proxy and native implementation cost. Under reconstruction constraints, finite-bond refinement selects the candidate that minimizes the worst-subcircuit discrepancy between normalized MPS simulations at bond dimensions $χ$ and $2χ$. Across 14 exhaustively enumerated small-scale candidate spaces, EP-Cost-guided genetic algorithm selections attain co-optimal native quasiprobability decomposition (QPD) overhead, with a median within-circuit Spearman correlation of 0.9965 between EP-Cost and QPD overhead. At matched cut counts, EP-Cost reduces QPD overhead by $25.20\times$ and $16.89\times$ for 8- and 9-qubit QFT circuits, respectively, and improves sampled reconstruction. On 12-qubit approximate QFT, deterministic reconstruction reduces Pauli-observable mean absolute error in all six low-bond conditions and total-variation distance in five. With five cuts and 7,776 QPD terms, SACE is best on all four reconstruction metrics in four of six conditions. At 24 qubits, SACE improves exact-local QFT accuracy in all 30 instance-bond-dimension conditions against each EP-Cost-only, Kernighan-Lin, and spectral baseline. The 50-qubit QFT comparisons remain favorable as reference convergence improves. Weighted QAOA circuits provide complementary, more bond-sensitive evidence. Refinement benefits persist across three search methods; larger candidate panels improve the optimized proxy without guaranteeing lower exact-local error.

Quantum machine learning offers new approaches to multivariate event classification in high-energy physics, but its performance relative to optimized classical methods remains uncertain under limited training data and restricted quantum resources. We investigate the sample-size scalability and representation sensitivity of a quantum support vector machine and a variational quantum classifier for Higgs-boson signal--background discrimination using simulated events from the ATLAS Higgs Machine Learning Challenge. The models are trained on balanced subsets ranging from 250 to 4000 events and evaluated on held-out samples preserving the original class distribution. Principal component analysis (PCA) and autoencoder (AE) representations are examined to reduce the input dimensionality and quantum-resource requirements. Four optimized classical classifiers are used as benchmarks under a common preprocessing, validation, and evaluation protocol. Within the evaluated Higgs-boson classification setting, the QSVM showed stronger performance than the VQC at larger training sample sizes, while remaining below the best-performing classical classifiers.

Fast continuous ion transport through X-junctions with low motional excitation for QCCD architectures

Trapped ion quantum computing based on the quantum charge coupled device (QCCD) architecture uses functional zoning and ion transport to enable high fidelity computation and all to all qubit connectivity. As trapped ion quantum processing units (QPU) scale, 90° X-junction turns account for an increasing fraction of total transport time. Frequent junction crossings, therefore, make transport speed and final phonon excitation direct determinants of quantum algorithm execution efficiency. However, complex electric fields in X-junctions complicate waveform design, making it difficult to achieve high speed and low final phonon excitation simultaneously. Here, we develop a waveform design method for low-phonon-excitation high-speed X-junction(LHX-T) transport. It integrates low barrier pseudopotential channel design using multiple radio frequency (Multi-RF), multidimensional shortcut to adiabaticity dynamics, and constrained DC voltage optimization over the entire transport sequence. This framework connects waveform design, control, and computational validation, with smooth multichannel waveforms computed rapidly through a Karush-Kuhn-Tucker(KKT) linear system. The resulting waveform transports a single $^{171}Yb^+$ ion through a 90° turn in $5μs$ at an average speed of 14.6 m/s. Over 50 consecutive round trips, the final equivalent coherent phonon number consistently stays below 0.001, with no noticeable accumulation over time. System level evaluations at the QCCD scale show that the median duration of a single execution cycle is reduced by about $30\%$ across various circuit types. The fraction of execution time spent on transport decreases from approximately $70\%$ to $55\%$. This approach offers a means of designing fast ion transport waveforms with low phonon excitation for scalable QCCD based QPUs and provides a technical basis for efficient execution of quantum algorithms.

Harmonic Reduction of Spin-Coupled Grover Walks on Hypercubes via Quantum Bernoulli Noises

We consider spin-coupled Grover walks on hypercubes within the framework of quantum Bernoulli noises (QBN). We identify parity-dependent harmonic subspaces generated by the central Hamming layers: in even dimension they give a fixed-point subspace, whereas in odd dimension the walk reduces on the harmonic subspace to an explicit eight-dimensional reduced dynamics. Using symmetric-group representation theory, we characterize the corresponding isotypic structure and determine the reduced spectrum. The harmonic reduction also yields explicit long-time probability behavior and shows that the resulting states may occupy an asymptotically vanishing fraction of the hypercube while still spanning its full Hamming diameter.

Quantum-Fisher-information-aware optimization yields a quantum reservoir advantage over tuned classical reservoirs

We show that a quantum reservoir computer optimized with its quantum Fisher information (QFI) has a quantum advantage over a classical reservoir of equal readout cost, provided by a quantum-information advantage: optimizing the quantum parameters with the QFI metric. On Mackey-Glass forecasting, a driven Jaynes-Cummings reservoir with one to three atoms beats a tuned echo-state network at every size from 4 to 30 trained weights, by 25-69% in held-out error, and is ahead of 99.5% of 10,000 random networks. After grid and Bayesian searches saturate, the QFI-aware stage lowers the error by 29-51% at 12-30 weights, beyond equal-budget black-box search or classical-parameter gradients alone, and at 30 weights turns a loss into a win. Quadrature-detected zero-to-ultralow-field nuclear magnetic resonance

We present the concept of quadrature-detected zero-to-ultralow-field nuclear magnetic resonance (QD ZULF NMR). Two orthogonal sensors, atomic optically pumped magnetometers, are used to separately detect components of oscillating sample magnetization, which are then combined into a quadrature signal. This approach enables the distinction of positive and negative resonance frequencies with respect to the laboratory frame---such that absolute signs of gyromagnetic ratios can be determined. In addition, QD ZULF NMR reduces peak density and overlap compared to ZULF NMR with single-axis detection, while providing richer information about nuclear-spin dynamics. Here, we realize a continuously operating molecular magnetic amplifier. Exponential Quantum Advantage in Stationary Signal Sensing

Sensing time-varying signals is essential for precision measurement and for characterizing fluctuations in physical systems. In this work, we show that quantum entanglement provides an exponential advantage in the acquisition time required to learn the temporal statistics of a stationary classical signal. The task is to estimate the characteristic function of joint displacements on selected bosonic modes within a fixed-duration window, using continuous measurements of one signal realization. We present an entanglement-assisted scheme based on two-mode squeezed probes and local Bell measurements with protected idlers, and bound its acquisition time accounting for readout noise and correlations between signal windows. For a strictly stationary pulse family, it achieves polynomial acquisition time at inverse-polynomial accuracy with logarithmic squeezing and polynomial photon number. On the other hand, we prove that any local idler-free protocols require acquisition time exponential in the number of selected temporal modes per window. This lower bound holds for both sequentially prepared and arbitrarily temporally entangled probes, allowing non-Gaussian inputs and measurements and causal classical feedback, as long as each output is measured destructively within its time bin. These results establish an exponential quantum learning advantage for a single continuously monitored stationary signal, with the sensing cost measured by total acquisition time.

The GHZ Game in Black and White

We show that it is possible for three people to win the GHZ game with just the standard real valued misty state formalism, but each party will need to have two balls instead of one. That is, there will be six balls involved, rather than three. In fact, as a general rule, instead of using complex numbers in a quantum computer you can always add a qubit to "tag" complex numbers. But for proofs of non-locality, like the GHZ game, we cannot have a single universal tagging qubit, because all three parties would need access to it and therefore they could potentially transfer information via it. That's where we need to replace the one ball with three that each only need to be accessed locally.

Competitive scaling of small-subsystem-optimization heuristics for LABS

The low autocorrelation binary sequences (LABS) problem, often formulated as a spin-lattice energy-minimization problem, is a combinatorial optimization problem with a very rugged energy landscape. We demonstrate that iterative optimization of very small subsystems of n spins that are decoupled from the rest of the system achieves global optimization of LABS with a time-to-solution scaling that is competitive with heuristics that optimize the global system of N spins directly. Our isolated-subsystem heuristic with n = 4 achieves a time scaling of O(1.333^N ) on the interval N = [27, 60], while the current state-of-the-art classical and quantum scalings are respectively O(1.34^N ) and O(1.21^N ). Our results suggest that specialized optimization hardware with only small numbers of logical spins may be of practical relevance.

Quantum Signal Processing Phase Factorization via Logarithmic Derivatives

Quantum signal processing (QSP) requires the efficient computation of phase factors associated with a polynomial transformation. One existing stable factorization method avoids direct root finding but relies on Prony's method to recover a polynomial from weighted power sums of its roots (arXiv:2202.02671). We present a modification of this approach that eliminates Prony's method by using the logarithmic derivative of an associated polynomial. Because the logarithmic derivative has the same residue at every root after accounting for multiplicity, its Fourier coefficients directly yield the unweighted power sums of the roots inside the unit disk. The Newton--Girard identities then recover the coefficients of the corresponding polynomial without computing its roots. The resulting algorithm has $O(d\log^2 d)$ computational complexity and simplifies the factorization procedure of the original method. In numerical experiments on Hamiltonian simulation, eigenstate filtering, matrix inversion, and the Fermi--Dirac operator, our implementation computes phase factors in seconds with maximum errors on the order of $10^{-12}$.

Lifting an $[n,k,d]$ Binary Linear Code to an $[[n,k,d]]$ Quantum Stabilizer Code

The codeword stabilized (CWS) framework provides a systematic method for constructing quantum error correcting codes from a simple undirected graph and a classical code. When the underlying classical code is linear, the resulting quantum code is a stabilizer code. In this work, we investigate the conditions under which an arbitrary $[n,k,d]$ classical binary linear code can be lifted to an $[[n,k,d]]$ quantum stabilizer code using the CWS framework. Compass in the Mirror: Quantum Backpropagation with the Hopf Ansatz

A variational quantum state may have many control angles, and its complete gradient contains one derivative for each. The task is to turn access to selected derivatives into a shared finite-shot record for the complete vector. The Hopf ansatz supplies the setting: it constructs any normalized pure state from nested two-way magnitude splits and, for complex states, leaf phases, with one directly programmed angle per split or phase. Earlier Hopf constructions prepare the state, recover coordinates from known amplitudes, and provide indexed derivative access. With a phase-calibrated controlled reflection, the inverse Hopf frame maps each normalized magnitude direction to a computational label, so every all-$X$ outcome contributes to every magnitude derivative. A separate signed leaf record supplies the complex phase block. A checkpoint variant reverses only the suffix below a selected depth and returns that layer's gradient block. For a complete chart with $M$ real-valued parameters, sufficient execution counts are $\mathcal{O}(\log\log M)$ for the global method and $\mathcal{O}(\log M\log\log M)$ for the complete checkpoint schedule at fixed additive coordinate accuracy and confidence. The global saving is record reuse, not a reduction of the $M$-entry output. Under the designated direct-angle schedules, the global method's assigned Hopf CNOT work and input/output-inclusive storage have at most logarithmic overhead relative to a matched scalar evaluation. The same Hopf geometry thus carries a state forward and the objective's complete coordinate response backward.

Semiclassical state on algebra of observables for de Sitter space and time reversal symmetry

In de Sitter static patch holography, coupling bulk quantum fields to an observer's dynamical clock yields a type II$_1$ von Neumann algebra $\mathcal{M}_+$ of gauge-invariant observables. However, the unique tracial state on this algebra fails to reproduce semiclassical bulk correlation functions. We resolve this issue by introducing a family of heavy-clock states, representing an observer reference frame with heavy inertia. We prove that in the heavy-clock limit, $n$-point correlation functions of gauge-invariant dressed observables reproduce the bulk quantum field correlation functions on de Sitter spacetime. Furthermore, we investigate a generalized observer Hamiltonian $\widehat{H}_{\rm obs}=|\widehatπ|$ that incorporates both forward-running ($π\geq 0$) and backward-running ($π<0$) clocks. The resulting von Neumann algebra $\mathcal{M}$ of gauge-invariant observables remains a type~II$_1$ factor and is isomorphic to $\mathcal{M}_+\otimes M_2(\mathbb{C})$, where $M_2(\mathbb{C})$ is the algebra of $2\times2$ complex matrices transforming between the two clock orientations. Time-reversal symmetry is implemented in this setting as an anti-unitary operator that exchanges the two clock orientations, yielding a type~II$_1$ von Neumann algebra $\mathcal{F}$ of gauge-invariant, time-reversal-invariant observables. We generalize the heavy-clock state construction to both $\mathcal{M}$ and $\mathcal{F}$, showing that $n$-point correlation functions calculated in heavy-clock states match the corresponding semiclassical bulk correlation functions. Finally, by coupling Einstein gravity to the Brown--Kuchař dust field, we derive the observer Hamiltonian $\widehat{H}_{\rm obs}=|\widehatπ|$ from a perturbative reduction along the observer's worldline in the $G \to 0$ limit. This provides a field-theoretic foundation for the observer model developed in this work.

Experimental observation of Higgs oscillations and their power-law decay across pairing regimes in a cavity QED quantum simulator

Understanding how pairing correlations in superconductors evolve out of equilibrium is a central challenge in quantum many-body physics. In systems with $s$-wave pairing, the character of Cooper pairing evolves continuously from a BCS regime of weakly bound, overlapping pairs to a BEC regime of tightly bound molecules, each with distinct excitation spectra. Here we realize and probe pairing dynamics across these regimes using cold, non-degenerate $^{87}$Rb atoms coupled to an optical cavity with Floquet dressing. Our cavity-QED platform emulates fermionic superfluids through a mapping onto Anderson pseudospins, while optical and microwave control tunes the ratio of interactions to single-particle energies, providing a flexible approach complementary to ultracold Feshbach and ultrafast solid-state platforms. Following a quench, we observe persistent pairing and transient oscillations of the order parameter associated with a collective amplitude (Higgs) mode. We find that the decay of these oscillations sharply distinguishes weak- and strong-pairing regimes: in the weak-pairing regime, oscillations decay with a power-law exponent $|α|=0.65(8)$, whereas in the strong-pairing regime the decay is substantially faster, with $|α|>1$. The oscillation frequency is set by the minimum quasiparticle excitation energy, directly linking nonequilibrium dynamics to the underlying excitation spectrum. Our results establish nonequilibrium dynamics as a direct probe of pairing character and collective excitations in interacting quantum systems.

Categorifying Quantum Spin Systems

We develop a homotopical framework for understanding the space of quantum spin systems based on topological $n$-nets, which assign associative unital algebras to local regions in an $n$-dimensional space in a manner that captures the locality structure of the underlying lattice theories. The net-theoretic perspective admits a natural categorification, in which the category of topological $n$-nets carries the structure of a higher Morita category. Moreover, the nets of greatest physical interest form a Karoubi-complete subcategory that shares many of the characteristic structural features of the category of finite TQFTs. Our main result identifies the group of locality-preserving symmetries of (3+1)-dimensional quantum spin systems (i.e. quantum cellular automata modulo stabilization, finite-depth quantum circuits, and translations) with the full Witt group of nondegenerate braided fusion categories, thereby answering a question posed by Haah.

An entangling Hubbard quasiparticle collider on a digital quantum processor

Scattering of quasiparticles provides direct access to the interactions that govern strongly correlated systems. In this work, we realize the quasiparticle scattering experiment of the Fermi-Hubbard model on a digital quantum processor by colliding a doublon and a holon in the half-filled chain of 21 sites encoded on 42 qubits of the ibm_boston processor. For the first time, we measure the transmission probability of quasiparticles of the Hubbard model on quantum hardware, which agrees with the Bethe-ansatz scattering matrix without free parameters. Beyond the scattering probabilities, the collision generates entanglement by superposing the transmitted and reflected components, which we infer from the particle-number fluctuation. In sharp contrast, a control collision between two doublons shows neither the interaction dependence nor the number fluctuation, as expected for indistinguishable particles. Our work demonstrates that the ability to create quasiparticles of a chosen flavor on demand and program their motion and interactions makes a digital quantum processor a new experimental toolset for probing the entanglement structure generated by quasiparticle scattering.

A Warped Born Rule Turns an Observer into a Maxwell's Demon

Quantum mechanics seems to be an incredibly delicate theory. Slight alterations to nearly any part of its structure can lead to huge qualitative differences and wildly unphysical predictions. Here, we explore the many-body consequences of replacing the Born rule $p(m)\propto\|P_m|ψ\rangle|^2$ by a warped rule $p_b(m)\propto\|P_m|ψ\rangle\|^{2b}$. Specifically, in systems with conserved particle number, evolution punctuated by repeated local particle observations acquires a probabilistic bias over measurement histories with an effect controlled by the sign of $b-1$. For $b>1$, this bias generically drives the gas to collect into nearly frozen configurations. Similar to the action of Maxwell's demon, the simple act of observation seems to extract entropy from a closed system. For $b<1$, the bias is reversed, generically scouring out long-wavelength fluctuations to produce a hyperuniform gas. We study this effect in a one-dimensional quantum gas, where these two phases are derived analytically for a particular measurement scheme and corroborated numerically for more general procedures with discrete-time projective-measurement circuit models using matrix product state simulations. We additionally discuss how this bias manifests in a common technique that often employs this warped rule, the ``replica trick,'' implying qualitative differences between finite-replica and replica-limit calculations.

A scalable sample-based quantum diagonalization solver for general Pauli-sum Hamiltonians

A scalable ground-state solver to be used in sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) is presented. The solver is built on JAX API, and is intended to be run on GPU platforms and possibly many-GPU high-performance computing facilities. It is capable of subspace diagonalization in up to $2^{30}$-dimensional subspace, with possible upgrades to enable even larger-scale operations. The two main components, a general-purpose eigensolver and an SQD-specific matrix-vector operation function, are described in detail. Efficiency of the eigensolver with respect to the GPU memory is demonstrated using a simple physics model. Furthermore, good strong scaling of the eigensolver in many-GPU environment is observed.
---
Quantum Query Complexity of Maximum Degree and its Generalizations

We study the quantum query complexity of several basic graph problems. 1) We prove a tight $Θ(n^{3/2})$ bound for finding a maximum-out-degree vertex in an $n$-vertex tournament, resolving a conjecture of Mande, Paraashar, and Saurabh [FSTTCS'23]. 2) Using the same proof idea, we give a direct proof that finding a maximum-degree vertex in an $n$-vertex undirected graph requires $Θ(n^{3/2})$ quantum queries, a bound implicit in the work of Apers and Lee [CCC'21]. Both lower bounds above hold even for computing only the maximum degree or maximum out-degree. 3) We also study Maximum $(k,n-k)$-Cut, which asks for the largest cut with one side of size $k$. For every constant $k\geq 1$, we give an $O(n^{2-1/(k+1)})$-query quantum algorithm. 4) We establish a reduction to Maximum $(k,n-k)$-Cut involving $k$-Graph Collision, which asks whether the marked vertices of a known graph contain a $k$-clique. For $k\geq 3$, this yields unconditional lower bounds with exponents strictly larger than $3/2$, showing that this problem is strictly harder than computing maximum degree. Additionally, under a conjecture we propose for $k$-Graph Collision, the reduction yields a lower bound matching our upper bound above.
---
Maximally antiflat fixed-particle-number fermionic states

Fermionic Gaussian states are completely characterized by their two-point correlations, making the covariance matrix a natural reference for quantifying non-Gaussian structure. Motivated by fermionic antiflatness, we study its extremal form for pure number-conserving states and ask which states can be maximally antiflat and how they can be constructed. We show that, at fixed particle number, maximal first antiflatness is equivalent to a completely flat one-body reduced density matrix, and we obtain a complete existence classification together with explicit block and cyclic-orbit constructions of all allowed sectors. We then investigate the structure that appears beyond the covariance level. Maximally antiflat states with identical one-body data can exhibit distinct connected four-point correlations, which we characterize through the four-point Majorana Wick error and, equivalently, the connected two-body reduced density matrix cumulant. We show that this quartic structure is necessarily nonzero and formulate its extremization as a two-body reduced density matrix (2RDM) purity problem. Combining this framework with a Hilbert--Schmidt estimate of Christiansen for truncated two-body correlations, we obtain an upper bound on the connected quartic structure in terms of first antiflatness. Our results establish both a complete characterization of maximal fermionic antiflatness at fixed particle number and a constrained hierarchy linking covariance-level flatness to higher-order non-Gaussian correlations.

An Annealing-based Quantum Neural Network with Discrete Wavelet Transforms for Enhanced Air Pollution Nowcasting

Air pollution poses a significant threat to public health, exacerbated by the rapid industrialization and urbanization of modern society. While traditional deep learning offers predictive capabilities, recent advancements in quantum machine learning (QML) present a compelling alternative. Here, we introduce a scalable hybrid framework that integrates variational quantum circuits with quantum annealing. Leveraging a neuromorphic quantum computing platform to transcend the qubit limitations of the NISQ era, we model particulate matter (PM$_{2.5}$) dynamics using high-density urban data from the UK. We demonstrate that our Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) exhibit an intrinsic resilience to input volatility, significantly outperforming classical baselines (MLP and LSTM) on raw, high-entropy environmental data. Furthermore, we successfully scaled the model to 172 qubits, observing a synergistic enhancement when coupled with wavelet decomposition that achieves state-of-the-art predictive fidelity. These findings establish a critical benchmark for quantum utility, proving that scalable, neuromorphic quantum machine learning can now address complex, non-linear real-world problems.

Bicycle Race: small two-block codes on the Pareto frontier

Two-block group-algebra codes are among the simplest quantum LDPC codes, and many of them are translation invariant on a twisted torus, which suits neutral-atom arrays and other platforms with movable qubits. We ask how far they go at small sizes when searched systematically across all groups. We use algebraic constraints and screens to design a search pipeline capable of enumerating broad and complete swathes of two-block codes on a laptop. With it we census about $10^8$ two-block codes with up to $320$ qubits at check weights five to nine. Several of these censuses are exhaustive, and support complete statements; for instance, the Gross code is, up to equivalence, the only abelian weight-six two-block code with $n \le 200$ to reach $kd^2/n=12$, no weight-six two-block code with $n \le 300$ beats $[[288,16,16]]$, and the known $[[64,18,8]]$ is the best two-block code by $kd^2/n$ across weights five to nine with $n \le 94$. The censuses are also large enough to give insights into where good codes live - they concentrate in a handful of groups; abelian groups give the best codes at small $n$ and non-abelian groups at large $n$, with the crossover appearing to move out as the check weight grows; and high quality codes become $100$ to $1000$ times rarer per draw from weight six to weights eight and nine. Using the group algebra and the census data to steer a statistical search finds four to fifteen times as many good codes as uniform draws in equal time. Among the codes found are $[[102,20,10]]$ and $[[120,24,10]]$ at check weight nine, which we simulate in noisy syndrome-extraction circuits, and $[[186,20,18]]$, a weight-nine code with every check in a $5\times5$ window. At check weights seven to nine, our best codes exceed the best published $kd^2/n$ with exact distance at the same check weight in most length bands up to $320$ qubits. All codes are available in a public database.

GPU-Based Parallel BP Decoding for Quantum LDPC Memory

Quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) decoding requires reliable corrections and low latency over long syndrome histories. We extend BP-SF, an oscillation-guided belief-propagation (BP) decoder with independent syndrome-flip trials, by introducing windowed and streaming operations. Our CUDA implementation combines disordered-memory retries, sliding windows, correlation-aware graphs, and layered scheduling. Circuit-level simulations test fixed-basis memory and complete logical Pauli-frame recovery. On the [[144,12,12]] bivariate bicycle code at physical error rate p = 0.002, parallel BP-SF reduces 99.9th-percentile call latency by a factor of 13.15 relative to an eight-chain Relay-BP GPU baseline on 100,000 matched samples for which every tested method's initial BP stage fails within 80 iterations. Windowed BP-SF maintains a flat logical error rate and nearly constant per-round compute time through 512 rounds, reaching a 17.1-fold speedup over monolithic decoding. In 512-round full-frame streaming decoding with 2.97-ms syndrome arrivals, layered BP-SF on a transformed joint graph with 20 retries reduces 99.9th-percentile correction-release age from 166.29 to 36.25 ms relative to the joint graph with flooding updates and 100 retries. These results connect parallel BP post-processing to lower decoding costs and earlier correction release in streaming quantum memory.

No advantage from classical assistance for energy-constrained bosonic dephasing channels

We prove that four capacities of single-mode bosonic dephasing channels coincide under an average photon-number constraint: the unassisted quantum capacity, the unassisted private capacity, the two-way classically assisted quantum capacity, and the two-way public-classically assisted private capacity. The assistance may be interactive: the parties can exchange classical messages between channel uses and choose subsequent inputs based on earlier outcomes. For every finite value of the constraint, their common value is the coherent information optimized over Fock-diagonal input states satisfying the constraint. The proof uses a relative entropy comparison with the completely dephasing channel to derive chain rules for the Rains relative entropy and the relative entropy of entanglement. These chain rules give an upper bound on the rates achievable by adaptive protocols whose mean input photon number is constrained on average over all channel uses. We also show that both chain rules are attained by canonical purifications of finite-energy Fock-diagonal states.

Enhanced analogue quantum computation with a logically-encoded XX gadget

The introduction of two-body XX interactions has been shown to improve the performance of quantum annealing in solving certain combinatorial optimisation problems and would enable the simulation of a broader class of many-body Hamiltonians. Due to experimental constraints, such interactions are typically not natively available on analogue quantum devices. To overcome this limitation we introduce the truss gadget. This is an exact construction that generates two-body XX interactions using only one-body X and Z fields and two-body ZZ interactions which are readily accessible on, for instance, superconducting quantum annealers. We experimentally validate the construction by reproducing the dynamics of a target XX Hamiltonian on a trapped-ion quantum processor. We then apply the gadget to an illustrative set of instances of the weighted maximum-independent-set problem on a superconducting quantum annealer. We empirically determine an approximate thousand-fold improvement in the probability of finding a state close to the ground state for these instances, over implementation without an XX interaction.
